Football comes together across Spain to honour Jesús de Miguel: ‘We’ve seen how fragile life is’

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Across La Liga and beyond this weekend, footballers took to the field knowing it could have been them

In the final few minutes before stepping into the arena once more, heading out for his 199th Osasuna match on Saturday evening, the man who has scored more first division goals than anyone in their history stopped his teammates for a moment and told them what it meant to wear the shirt. “This is very important,” Ante Budimir began as the dressing room, their sanctuary, fell silent around him. “We don’t have this shirt today for the sake of it. He was not chosen, and nor are we. It could have happened to anyone, anyone. Let us go knowing that we’re grateful to be on the pitch: playing, watching, warming up, sitting on the bench, whatever it may be. Boys, we were not chosen: not us, not him. Let us go, gratefully. Without thought. We have a grand day before us.”

With that, Osasuna’s players turned left out of the room, where they were joined in the tunnel by footballers from Athletic Club, rivals from the north, and together they went out past the little shrine of Saint Fermin on to the field. They carried white lilies and all wore the same white shirts with a No 9 on the back, a black ribbon and the name of Jesús de Miguel, the Tenerife striker who died after a collision on the pitch five days earlier at the age of 30. At almost exactly the same time, 2,000km away to the west, Tenerife’s squad were walking out at the Heliodoro Rodríguez López in Santa Cruz. They did not have anyone alongside them, wore black suits not white shirts and did not have a game to play either, not this week.

Two days earlier Tenerife had been in Navahermosa, La Mancha, the tiny village their teammate’s family are from, carrying the coffin of the friend their captain called “our Demigol”. They were supposed to be in Granada on Saturday; instead, they were back on the island, in their home ground, to say goodbye and pay homage. Publicly, this time. Lilies had been laid on the pitch there too, a Tenerife shirt with them, and a stage built at one end. “We’ve seen how fragile life is,” the captain, Enric Gallego, said, offering words he said could never be enough, carrying the burden for all of them. “We think of the journeys, the wins, the losses, and hold on to those moments that seemed normal, but that now … a few days ago we shared that with you. To talk of you in the past tense is unfair. None of us are prepared to live what we’re living through.”

No one ever could be. Across Spain this weekend, footballers took to the field knowing it could have been them. That, Budimir had told his teammates, was something they must think about but not think about too. The players’ union in which De Miguel had a role, a footballer there for others, had asked for the competition to stop. Respect demanded that it did, they said, requesting a judge intervene when the league did not hear them. But it had gone on. The only matches postponed were those involving Tenerife; Andorra, whose goalkeeper Jesús Owono had crashed into De Miguel; and Castellón, who he had taken to the second division, delivering them to segunda for the first time in 16 years and himself for the first time at all, aged 27.

On the eve of the game that opened this weekend’s fixtures, the Sevilla head coach, Luis García, said he thought they should wait. It was too soon, too raw, too theirs. His midfielder Giorgi Kochorashvili had missed training the day before: a former Castellón player, Kochorashvili had asked permission to go to the funeral, leaving early and returning late, his mind elsewhere. Of course he could, García had replied, “we are humans above all else”. And it was not just him. García had worked with Owono – “let’s not make two victims, we have had enough; he is in hell, for sure”, he had pleaded – and now he said: “If there’s a play that’s similar … what do I say to my goalkeeper?

“We need time,” García had said. “We need to reflect.”

Instead, they played. From Seville on Friday night to Madrid on Sunday, via stadiums everywhere. It was, some suggested, the best way to pay homage to De Miguel but no one truly knew. “We can’t ask him what he would have wanted,” José Mourinho said before Madrid went to Valencia and won 3-0, Tenerife’s flag flying at half-mast at Mestalla. He might not have wanted anything at all. “You never wanted any attention,” Gallego said, but this weekend he was on all their minds.

“Forever in our memories,” the league’s line read. At every game in the first and second divisions, the ritual was repeated. Teams came out together wearing the same white shirts bearing his name. Flowers were laid, a little uncomfortably, on the spot inside the centre circle where he had fallen and there was a minute’s silence, players and officials gathered around the wreath. As they stood before Cádiz-Oviedo, tears ran down the cheeks of the Tenerife-based referee Marta Huerta de Aza. On stadium screens, an image of De Miguel appeared. On television screens, a QR code did: a link to a crowdfunding campaign for the young family he left, set up by the former Tenerife player Fran Sol.

The games began and then, in the ninth minute or as near as the referee could manage, they paused again: the teams stopped, looking up to the screen, where a video of his best moments played out. It was not always an easy watch, and some preferred not to.

At Sevilla, where fans saw Antonio Puerta collapse on the pitch and pass away, applause still offered in the 16th minute every game, Kochorashvili stood by the bench, eyes lost somewhere his mind was not. At Espanyol, where in the 21st minute they applaud Dani Jarque, the captain who died during a pre-season and to whom Andrés Iniesta dedicated the winning goal at the 2010 World Cup final, Álex Calatrava and Álvaro González, teammates at Castellón and Tenerife respectively, looked about to break. In the ninth minute at the Metropolitano, the place was still rocking, the timing out of sync: Giuliano Simeone had just scored, so the referee Javier Alberola Rojas allowed a couple of minutes to pass, choosing his moment, Diego Simeone helping him impose silence on the stands when he finally stopped it. Then they started again, expected to carry on regardless.

In Pamplona, Alejandro Catena, who scored the late equaliser in a 1-1 draw with Athletic, raised an Osasuna shirt with De Miguel’s name and number on the back. Theirs was the game that felt most out of place, attention drawn from where it should have been, where it was. It would not be true to say that it did not matter, the Osasuna head coach, Jagoba Arrasate, admitting he had been carried along with it like everyone else, the calmness he thought they had found in the months out of the game soon abandoned again.

As they played, grateful for the grand day that they had before them, all the days they did, the tribute continued in Tenerife. The Heliodoro had filled early, access by every gate except eight and nine, engulfed by flowers and candles. De Miguel’s shirt lay on the turf where he had, lilies to one side, a spotlight on it. Another wreath occupied the bench. A giant shirt stretched across one end where the stage was, a lectern awaiting the speakers. A cross presided over it. Staff and players wore No 9 badges on their lapels. Supporters came in Tenerife shirts. And in the yellow shirts of rivals Las Palmas, whose president and captain Kirian, who had recovered after cancer, came. “This is the Canaries I want,” said Tenerife’s majority shareholder, Rayco García.

In the seats set up on the pitch, Tenerife’s players and De Miguel’s family sat, their eyes heavy. The archbishop, Eloy Alberto Santiago led a mass. The club’s president and majority shareholder spoke. He promised they would never allow María, De Miguel’s widow, to be alone. He also announced they would adopt Carla, his 14-month-old daughter, supporting her economically until she is 18. Gallego addressed her, football as family meaning something. “I hope one day you can look back and see who your dad was, be proud of him, not just for the footballer he was but the person: for his humility, his goodness,” he said.

Sniffing, his voice breaking, the captain paid homage to his teammate, his strike partner, his friend. The kind of person, he said, who did not seem to be there, and definitely did not demand that people notice he was, but always was. A man, he noted, so disinclined to put anyone out that when his car broke down he walked home from training rather than bother anyone for a lift. That, he insisted, might not seem like much, but it said much. “Today we don’t want to talk only of the player; we want to talk about you, Jesús, about the person that was behind the shirt,” he had begun. “Above any game, any team, what really remains is the person and the impact you leave. And you were a very special person.”

Gallego gave way to the archbishop, orations led by Tenerife staff, and as the act came to a close, difficult days ahead, the spotlight taken elsewhere, the drums began and the fans belted out a familiar tune, once more with more meaningful than ever before:

double quotation markWe have to sing with even more strength if you’re losing Even those who left us sing, from heaven This will never stop We will sing to eternity.”

Then Tenerife’s players departed. They have training on Monday and next week they play.

Congress returns with government funding, Pete Hegseth and the debt limit on the agenda

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The House and Senate are due to return to Washington on Monday with a crowded legislative agenda in the final weeks before the end of the 119th Congress.

Congress will need to act quickly to extend government funding, which expires Dec. 11. Lawmakers will need to pass the National Defense Authorization Act, and they may tackle ambitious proposals including a construction permitting package and a bill to regulate name, image and likeness rules in college sports.

The work will commence under a new dynamic. Republicans come back to the Capitol diminished after suffering substantial losses in this week's midterms. Democrats swept to a commanding majority in the House with at least 255 seats in the 435-member chamber and could emerge with as many as 56 seats in the Senate.

With majorities in both chambers looming, Democrats will have little incentive to cut deals with their GOP counterparts.

The most pressing concern lawmakers will face when they return is funding the government. Current funding expires Dec. 11. Congress must pass another continuing resolution to retain current spending levels or a full-year suite of appropriations bills before then to avoid a government shutdown.

Appropriations bills and continuing resolutions require bipartisan majorities due to the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold. Democrats are unlikely to negotiate a full year of government funding before the new Congress is sworn in, given that they will soon hold commanding majorities in both chambers and can use that to leverage concessions from the White House.

"This former preschool teacher is going to teach President Trump a simple lesson: The power of the purse belongs to CONGRESS!" Sen. Patty Murray, D-Wash., the presumptive chair of the Appropriations Committee, said in an X post Tuesday as election results rolled in. "It's NOT Trump's money. Taxpayer dollars belong to the American PEOPLE."

Adding another wrinkle to the funding push is the electoral loss of Sen. Susan Collins, R-Maine, who chairs the Senate Appropriations Committee and is one of the "four corners" of spending talks. Collins lost reelection to Democrat Troy Jackson, ending a five-term career in the Senate.

Some lawmakers, including Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., have called for Congress to raise the debt limit before Republicans lose control.

In September, the federal debt hit $40 trillion for the first time, bumping up against the $41.1 trillion ceiling written into the GOP's 2025 budget reconciliation law known as the "one big beautiful bill."

The Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the debt limit, said Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by mid-summer. The U.S. may no longer be able to meet all its obligations by November 2027, according to a Bank of America estimate from Nov. 6.

But Democrats may instead prefer to use the debt limit as a bargaining chip in the next Congress.

Republicans could theoretically raise the debt ceiling in a budget reconciliation package, as they did with their 2025 law. But cobbling together a post-election coalition to clear another reconciliation bill with exceptionally narrow margins would be difficult. Budget reconciliation, which allows party-line bills to skirt the filibuster in the Senate, requires near-total unity.

Congressional leadership, particularly on the Republican side, is likely to have difficulty with lawmaker attendance after dozens of members lost their seats in an electoral bloodbath. Leadership will also struggle to whip members into line, especially those who do not have another election to run.

Top senators on energy committees struck a deal on a permitting bill before the election. That accord to make construction easier could now be in jeopardy, though the Senate plans to hold a procedural vote to advance it Monday evening, according to a Senate aide familiar with the schedule. The person asked not to be named because the move has not been announced.

The resounding majorities Democrats won in both the House and Senate may give them incentive to torpedo the permitting deal in hopes of securing a better one later. The deal also may have been in trouble prior to the election, with top Democrats in the House not supporting the version their counterparts in the Senate accepted.

Politico reported Thursday that Democratic Minority Leader and likely next House Speaker Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., did not support the permitting deal.

Lawmakers have long sought, and failed to reach, a bipartisan, bicameral deal on permitting reform. Democrats want to speed up the buildout of clean energy. Republicans have long complained that the federal permitting process and environmental laws that comprise it stymie the U.S.'s ability to build critical infrastructure.

The House passed a version of the NDAA, which would authorize more than $1 trillion in defense spending, in July. But Senate Democrats blocked their chamber's version of the bill that same month over concerns about the Iran war.

"Donald Trump is dragging America deeper into a war in Iran with no authorization, no plan, and no exit strategy. Democrats will not go along," Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., said in a statement at the time.

The NDAA is typically seen as a must-pass, bipartisan bill.

The legislation as it stood in July also drew pushback from businesses over a provision that would restrict Pentagon contractors from executing stock buybacks and paying dividends unless they have the Defense Department's approval.

A new wrinkle threatens to derail the NDAA process during the lame duck, however. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth faces a vote on articles of impeachment upon Congress' return, and Democrats have already been highly skeptical of the Pentagon's asks in this year's budget as the expensive Iran war drags on.

Other priorities could also get floor time.

The Senate in September approved, in a bipartisan vote, a package that would establish federal rules for the ways in which college athletes are compensated. But that legislation has many opponents in the House, particularly among Democrats in the chamber, making its passage more complicated.

There's also been a yearslong effort to ban members of Congress from owning or trading individual stocks.

In previous Congresses, the effort was stymied by leadership from both parties. And more recently, a bipartisan push to pass legislation was derailed after House Democrats demanded the ban also apply to the president.

One GOP-led proposal, called the Stop Insider Trading Act, passed the House with some bipartisan support in July, but met with Democratic opposition because it would apply only to Congress and would not require lawmakers to divest existing holdings.

Sen. Todd Young, R-Ind., called for action on the issue in an X post Thursday. Young is a co-sponsor of the Senate version of the Stop Insider Trading Act.

"We have a great opportunity in the lame duck session to restore a small amount of trust by banning Members of Congress from trading stocks. I've been talking about this for many months, and it should be a no-brainer," Young said.

Democrats, however, are eager to claim a victory on congressional stock trading in the next Congress.

Jeffries, in a letter to fellow House members announcing his candidacy for speaker, listed it as a priority as part of Democrats' anti-corruption agenda.

"We must root out corrupt price gouging, clean up the mess at the Supreme Court and ban Members of Congress from trading stocks. Accountability begins at home," Jeffries wrote.

This tiny inkwell contains an alphabetic inscription in ancient Etruscan, a language that has never been fully decoded.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The chicken-shaped, black-glazed vase has an abecedarium ‪—‬ an inscription with the 26 letters of the Etruscan alphabet in order.

What it is: A bird-shaped ceramic inkwell

This small terra-cotta vase, which is shaped like a cockerel or young male chicken with human feet, was discovered in central Italy and was made by the ancient Etruscans. The potter inscribed the Etruscan alphabet on the body of the chicken, giving modern researchers clues to a language that has never been fully deciphered.

The vase stands just over 4 inches (10 centimeters) tall and is in the collection of the Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York City. The dark-black ceramic, known as bucchero, was made through a unique firing method that prevented the kiln from getting enough oxygen, which caused the clay to absorb carbon. Bucchero is considered the trademark Etruscan ceramic, and it was produced from the seventh to the fourth centuries B.C. by this pre-Roman society. It is unclear where the cockerel vase was originally found, but it may have been discovered in a grave in one of the many cemeteries that surround Viterbo, which was originally an Etruscan town about 50 miles (80 kilometers) northwest of modern-day Rome.

According to The Met, the vase may have been a portable inkwell used by an ancient Etruscan who was learning how to write. The cockerel's head was the stopper, and it was loosely attached to the body by a cord through a set of loops. The inscription on the body of the vase is known as an abecedarium ‪—‬ the sequential list of letters in an alphabet.

The Etruscan alphabet is well known, since it was adopted from a regional version of the Greek alphabet in the early eighth century B.C. There were 26 letters in the Etruscan alphabet, but one of them in this abecedarium is incorrect. A close look at the end of the alphabet shows that someone inscribed an S twice; the second one should look more like an X, according to The Met.

The terracotta cockerel vase was one of several ancient Etruscan artifacts that helped experts partially decode the mysterious ancient language, as it preserved the order of the letters in the early alphabet, archaeologist Gisela Richter wrote in a 1930 catalog of The Met's classical artifacts.

Thanks to the adoption of the Greek alphabet, it's possible to fully sound out the Etruscan language. But although more than 10,000 Etruscan inscriptions have been found, the language is still only partially understood because those inscriptions are short and any lengthy Etruscan literature that once existed has been lost.

For more stunning archaeological discoveries, check out our Astonishing Artifacts archives.

Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

Lost Rubens sketches return to German castle after 80 years

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Peter Paul Rubens, Der Prophet Elias auf dem goldenen Wagen (The Prophet Elijah on the Golden Chariot, 1620/21)

Five oil sketches by Peter Paul Rubens will be shown together at the Ducal Museum in Gotha, Germany, for the first time since they were lost more than 80 years ago.

The five works depicting saints were part of a group of 22 sketches that Rubens painted on wood in 1620 and 1621 as preparation for the ceiling paintings of the Jesuit church in Antwerp, which was the biggest commission he had received by that time. The sketches are particularly important because the ceiling paintings, which were famous in the 17th century, are no longer in existence. The church was hit by a lightning bolt in 1718 and burned down.

The sketches were among the most valuable works in the collections at Schloss Friedenstein in Gotha, where they had been since at least the early 19th century; one had been there for three centuries. But by the end of the Second World War, all five had disappeared.

Three were taken by representatives of the ducal family—which had not been their owners since 1918. They returned to the castle as American and Soviet forces closed in on Gotha and, according to experts, removed truckloads of art in the last weeks of the war. Three sketches were transported to the city of Coburg for protection and from there, they were illicitly sold and ended up on the US art market. The two remaining studies were looted by the Red Army and were restituted to Gotha in 1959.

For many decades the three sold by the ducal family remained lost. Then in 2024, one of them, St. Gregory of Nazianzus, was returned by a museum in Buffalo, New York, with help from Christie’s. Now a fourth, The Prophet Elijah in the Golden Chariot, which ended up in a private US collection, has been recovered in a private settlement mediated by the Paris auction house Cabinet Turquin. The recovery was funded by the Ernst von Siemens Art Foundation, which frequently acquires art for German museums.

Peter Paul Rubens, De heilige Augustinus (Augustine of Hippo, 1620)

© Sammlung Emil Bührle, Dauerleighgabe im Kunsthaus Zurich

Martin Hoernes, the secretary general of the Ernst von Siemens Art Foundation, said a “figure in the low millions” had been agreed, below the market price. “No one wanted to criminalise the current owners,” he said, describing their acquisition of the sketch as a “good-faith purchase”.

The fifth sketch, showing St. Augustine, belongs to the Foundation E. G. Bührle Collection, which is on display in the Kunsthaus Zurich. The Bührle Foundation has agreed to loan it to Gotha’s Ducal Museum so that it can be shown alongside the other four for an exhibition, which opens today (9 November) and marks the 450th anniversary of Rubens’ birth next year.

“It is nothing short of a miracle that Peter Paul Rubens’ five oil studies can now be seen together at the Ducal Museum,” Tobias Pfeifer-Helke, the director of the Friedenstein Foundation Gotha, said in a statement. “They are not only five unique masterpieces by the Flemish painter and the jewels of the Gotha art collection, but also bear witness to European and international history.”

The dealer is showing a depiction of the Christian holy family at his fair booth, three decades after he hosted a show on the Old Master in New York

The work is on loan to the exhibition ‘Rembrandt 1632: The Creation of a Brand’ at the Ducal Museum Gotha

Flemish master had access to art from across the ages and assiduously reworked drawings by other artists

The oil sketch “Saint Gregory of Nazianzus” (1621) is one of five works by the Old Master that were unlawfully taken from the Friedenstein Foundation, with two still missing

Anchovy-Garlic Pasta

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Even those who swear they don’t like anchovies should be willing to take a “no thank-you” portion of this pasta. Sizzled with garlic, the tiny fish melt into a briny sauce that’s similar to bagna cauda, the warm Northern Italian dip whose savory quality comes from said anchovies. Instead of serving alongside crudités, toss this sauce with hot long pasta like linguine or spaghetti. No extra vegetables or protein needed, but I wouldn’t kick a salad with beans and bitter-slanting greens (kale, radicchio) off the table.

More weeknight-friendly pasta dinners this way.

Cook fillets and oil from two 2-oz. tins oil-packed anchovy fillets, ¼ cup extra-virgin olive oil, and ½ tsp. freshly ground pepper in a large high-sided skillet or medium Dutch oven over medium-low heat, breaking up fillets with a wooden spoon or heatproof rubber spatula, until anchovies are melted and mixture is bubbling, about 2 minutes. Add 6 garlic cloves, finely grated, and cook, stirring often, until fragrant, about 30 seconds. Remove pan from heat.

Cook 12 oz. long pasta (such as linguine or spaghetti) in a large pot of boiling salted water until very al dente, about 3 minutes less than package directions. Drain, reserving 2 cups pasta cooking liquid.

Gradually pour 1 cup pasta cooking liquid into anchovy mixture in pan, whisking constantly to incorporate. Place pan over high heat and bring sauce to a simmer. Add pasta and cook, tossing vigorously with tongs, until pasta is al dente and sauce is slightly thickened, about 2 minutes.

Remove pasta from heat and add 3 Tbsp. chilled unsalted butter, cut into pieces. Toss until butter is melted and pasta is coated and glossy, about 45 seconds. Add 1 cup coarsely chopped parsley, 1 Tbsp. finely grated lemon zest, and 3 Tbsp. fresh lemon juice and toss to combine, adding more pasta cooking liquid a splash at a time as needed to keep pasta saucy. Divide among shallow bowls to serve.

Oshinówò Studio tops Lutyens-designed war memorial with glass prism to honour Sierra Leone's Carrier Corps

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Photography by Tolu Sanusi / Oshinówò Studio unless stated

The Commonwealth War Graves Commission has unveiled a memorial to the Sierra Leone Carrier Corps in Freetown, Sierra Leone.

Officially opened this weekend, the memorial designed by Oshinówò Studio sits on top of a war memorial designed by British architect Edwin Lutyens in 1931.

While the original memorial honoured soldiers who died in the first world war, it did not include the names of the 946 members of the Sierra Leone Carrier Corps – men enlisted to transport supplies and materials – who also died in the war.

Oshinówò Studio extended the Lutyens-designed, stone memorial by adding a structural glass pyramid etched with the names of the Sierra Leone Carrier Corps men.

“The Freetown Memorial is conceived through a material symbolism that allows it to resonate across cultures and contexts,” said Oshinówò Studio founder Tosin Oshinówò.

“The structure we designed is, in effect, an extension of Sir Edwin Lutyens’ existing monument. By following its tapering geometry, the new monument rises higher, elevating the immense volume of names etched into glass. “

According to Oshinówò, placing the names of the carrier corps men higher than the soldiers was also a symbolic move.

“In this gesture, the Sierra Leone Carrier Corps are symbolically carried by their comrades, reversing the relationship between those who served and those who were served,” she said.

“There is a profound irony in this act: it becomes a fitting expression of gratitude to men whose sacrifice was fundamental to shaping the world we inhabit today.”

Along with the glass pyramid, the memorial also includes a laser beacon that will be illuminated on significant days of remembrance. According to the team, this column of light will be visible from up to five kilometres.

As part of the project, the 1930s memorial was also full restored by stonemasons and bronze specialists from the Commonwealth War Graves Commission.

Recent memorials featured on Dezeen include a “serene and contemplative” memorial to Queen Elizabeth II designed by Foster + Partners and a park in Ethiopia dedicated to the 157 victims of the Ethiopian Airlines Flight 302 crash.

Photography by Tolu Sanusi / Oshinówò Studio unless stated

Ziff Davis Says Organic Google Referrals Fell About 37% via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Calls from ChatGPT and Claude often get filed as direct. This checklist shows how to track AI search as its own call source.

Calls from ChatGPT and Claude often get filed as direct. This checklist shows how to track AI search as its own call source.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Guide: AI Max Drove 68% Of Retail Invalid Clicks

A large publisher says organic Google referrals fell again and pointed to Search Console's AI report. Here's what its AI impressions comparison measures.

Ziff Davis says organic Google referrals to its properties fell about 37% year over year in the third quarter. CEO Vivek Shah told analysts on the company’s Nov. 6 earnings call that referrals fell about 23% in the same quarter last year, putting the two-year decline at more than 50%.

Shah referenced an impressions figure from Google Search Console as well. Ziff Davis’ digital media sites generated about 2.7 billion impressions for both AI Overviews and AI Mode during the quarter, which was nearly 10 times “the clicks those same sites received from Google Search” based on his estimates. Other sites that have Search Console’s generative AI report are able to generate the same comparison. Google’s definitions show this compares impression counts inside their AI feature reporting vs. all click throughs from Search.

Shah said Ziff Davis is “not planning on a search recovery” and expects referrals to keep falling. Third-quarter revenue fell 5.6% to $289.3 million, a smaller drop he attributed to businesses that don’t rely on Google traffic, such as subscriptions and apps.

Shah said an impression is a count of “how many times a link to a page was shown inside generative AI features” and that this figure is coming from Search Console. Google has a help page to explain what the generative AI performance report does and it says the report counts the number of times links to a site appeared in AI Overviews and AI Mode. Since the report draws these two features from the Web search data within the main Performance report, AI impressions are a subset of a website’s total Search impression figures.

Google’s help page on how clicks are counted says a click on a link to another website within an AI Overview or in AI Mode counts towards a site’s total click count. None of the Search appearance types listed by Google for the Performance report includes either feature.

Shah did not say during the call where he got his click total from. If Shah used Search Console to get his total clicks from Google Search, then those clicks would include AI Overview clicks, AI Mode clicks and every other possible type of result in Search. This means that Shah’s ratio is comparing AI feature impressions to all clicks from Search. As of Nov. 8, Google documents no separate AI click count.

I covered how Search Console counts AI clicks last week, after Google’s Gary Illyes said the company had nothing to announce yet on adding more data.

Shah referred to “the disruption of search traffic by Google and LLMs” as a clear business headwind. He said that Ziff Davis’ tech and shopping brands “continue to face the strongest search traffic headwinds in our portfolio”; however, he also highlighted offset areas, such as social commerce and the relaunched RetailMeNot app.

Ziff Davis’ tech and shopping revenue declined 3.8% year over year during the quarter to $82 million (as reported in its earnings presentation). The decline has narrowed for three consecutive quarters per Shah’s account.

Shah said Ziff Davis’ largest property based on search referrals, IGN, has continued to hold up relatively well. He explained that “a lot of its search referral is around game help, which is very maps heavy” and that it is difficult to duplicate game maps. IGN also receives a significant amount of direct traffic.

Despite that, gaming and entertainment revenue declined 2.1%, and Shah attributed the decline to the poor gaming advertising environment leading up to the release of Grand Theft Auto VI later this month. He added that IGN’s business is significantly driven by video, including vertical and social video, versus web traffic.

In his comments to investors during the Ziff Davis earnings call in November 2025, Shah said AI Overviews were present in 29% of search queries that generate the majority of Ziff Davis’ traffic. While there had been a slight decrease in the percentage of searches containing AI Overviews, he was more concerned with overall Google search volatility than with AI Overviews themselves. His current figure for that quarter’s referral decline is about 23%.

In August, he said AI Overviews appeared on about 50% of the queries relevant to Ziff Davis’ properties, up from about 36% when he last gave the figure. He didn’t give an AI Overview share on the Nov. 6 call.

Shah said making money from Ziff Davis’ content in AI answers was “the priority”, and that they are open to licensing agreements. Additionally, he expects the pending litigation by Ziff Davis against OpenAI will help clarify this space. In his words, “there is a gap between usage and monetization” even as marketers give more weight to AI visibility when choosing media partners.

Ziff Davis plans for referrals to continue declining; Shah’s method will provide each of these sites that have the generative AI report with a large publisher’s number to place next to its own. Those sites can recreate the ratio by comparing their AI impression data to their web search click data for the same time period. Since clicks on AI link results are included with all other Search clicks, the result cannot be used as an AI click-through rate.

The UK’s Competition and Markets Authority has set a Dec. 3 deadline for Google to give publishers impressions, clicks and click-through rate for AI search features in the UK, aggregated across features such as AI Overviews and AI Mode, according to the regulator’s summary. As I reported last week, the CMA’s interpretive notes name Search Console as one example of where that data could appear.

Watch how Google provides those metrics around Dec. 3. If it reports AI clicks apart from other Search clicks, publishers could line them up with their AI impressions.

Expedia Goes Live on Muse, but Its AI Wins Are Inside Its Own Apps

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: Expedia's Muse integration is live Skift/Screenshot

Expedia's Muse connection is live: CEO Ariane Gorin noted it may even have happened during the OTA's earnings call this week. So Skift took it for a spin to see what it can and cannot do.

Meta’s Muse agent can now book Expedia hotels, about six weeks after the companies announced the integration. But there are limits.

While discussing third-quarter results last Wednesday, Expedia Group CEO Ariane Gorin teased the release, saying the connection was going live in the U.S., “potentially while we’re on this call today.”

In a follow-up, Expedia confirmed to Skift what the integration can and can’t do: It covers natural-language search and booking, but not cancellations or rebooking.

"Travelers can describe their destination, dates, and hotel preferences in their own words, and Muse can surface relevant hotel options from Expedia," a spokesperson said. "Because Expedia is the merchant of record, changes can be made through Expedia’s customer support, just like when a booking is made directly with us."

Muse offered more detail when Skift ran a limited test. In a chat, the agent explained that the integration can book only

Tags: artificial intelligence, expedia, meta, muse, online travel, online travel newsletter, skift intelligence, the prompt

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Meta's Muse AI agent can now book Expedia hotels in the U.S., roughly six weeks after the two companies announced the integration, but the connection is narrower than the announcement suggested. Expedia confirmed to Skift that Muse handles natural-language search and booking while cancellations and changes still route through Expedia customer support, since Expedia is the merchant of record. In a limited Skift test, Muse said it can only book properties with upfront "pay-now" pricing; pay-at-property listings require the agent to use its built-in browser on Expedia's site. Payment runs through Stripe Link, Meta's default option, even when a traveler links an Expedia account — though linking an account unlocks member pricing, which cut one listing's cost by more than $100 versus guest mode. Expedia is adding agentic channels quickly, including ChatGPT, Claude, and Google's AI Mode in the third quarter, and is packaging its technology as a B2B "template" with Qantas as the first partner. The volume isn't there yet: Expedia said it still isn't seeing meaningful traffic from AI, and Booking Holdings CFO Ewout Steenbergen said large language model referrals account for "significantly less than 1%" of Booking.com room nights. CEO Ariane Gorin said the integrations are valuable now mainly as a way to learn how agents make decisions and what tech they require, and that the small volume is high-intent traffic.

Father Arrested For Leaving 6-Year-Old Daughter Sleeping In Line For Pokémon Cards

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A 35-year-old South Carolina man has been arrested and accused of leaving his 6-year-old daughter asleep while waiting in line for Pokémon cards.

As reported by The Washington Post (thanks, IGN), John-Michael Dooley was waiting outside a Target store with his daughter in Charleston's West Ashley district, where a crowd had gathered hoping to purchase the newly-released Pokémon set. Officers arrived at the store at 2:30am local time after receiving reports of an unattended child.

Witnesses reported that Dooley gave his daughter a pillow and a blanket sometime after 1:00am before leaving her asleep in the line. After he failed to return within an hour, the concerned witnesses contacted the police.

When the man eventually returned to the store, he was arrested. He later admitted to leaving his daughter unattended so that he could keep his place in line while he had gone home. He was subsequently charged with unlawful conduct toward a child, while his daughter was placed in emergency protective custody. Thankfully, she was unharmed.

Many of us here at NL are parents, and so it enrages us to no end that someone would even consider doing this. We're at least relieved that no harm came to the 6-year-old girl, but it could have been much worse.

Alexis Mac Allister shares what one Crystal Palace player told him about Alisson on Sunday

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Alisson Becker’s outstanding performance in Liverpool’s 3-1 win away to Crystal Palace on Sunday was reflected in what one Eagles player told Alexis Mac Allister.

The Reds’ number 1 had shipped criticism in recent days for errors in the defeats to Arsenal and Fenerbahce, but he was back to his world-class best at Selhurst Park yesterday as he turned in a man-of-the-match display for his side.

The Brazilian pulled off six saves in total against Pierre Sage’s team, including a stunning reaction stop to keep out a fearsome volley from Daichi Kamada just a couple of minutes before the Argentine scored the visitors’ opening goal.

Speaking to BBC Sport after the match (via liverpoolfc.com), Mac Allister was singing Alisson’s praises and shared what the Japan international had told him about the Liverpool goalkeeper.

The Reds’ number 10 said of the Brazil stopper: “He’s so good, especially the save from Kamada’s shot [in the first half]. Kamada couldn’t believe the save he made and he was telling me, but that’s who he is.

“He is an amazing goalkeeper and an amazing guy as well. I’m really, really happy for him.”

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

Alisson had endured some unusually shaky moments in recent weeks, but he reminded us all on Sunday that class is permanent and that he remains pivotal to Liverpool’s hopes of success this season.

In addition to the outstanding save from Kamada, he also produced excellent stops to keep out an Adam Wharton header just before half-time and a double save from Yeremy Pino and Jefferson Lerma in the 77th minute when the Reds were 2-0 to the good.

That Palace scored just once from an xG of 2.6 yesterday sums up just how imperious our number 1 had been, and it’s no wonder that the Eagles’ Japanese attacker was stunned by the 34-year-old’s brilliance.

Such was Alisson’s performance at Selhurst Park that the Premier League’s official X profile posted a compilation of his saves from the win over the south London outfit, with the goalkeeper treating us to his own showreel.

Anyone who might’ve thought that the Brazil international was in irreversible decline was made to think again yesterday by a masterclass from Liverpool’s legendary number 1.

Strangers Raise $800,000 for Red Baron Worker Filmed Being Harassed by Prankster

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strangers Raise $800,000 for Red Baron Worker Filmed Being Harassed by Prankster

A Red Baron pizza worker who was filmed being harassed by a prankster pretending to steal $20 from him has paused donations on a GoFundMe page set up on his behalf after it neared $800,000.

The prank severely backfired on the young man filming the video after the Red Baron worker lost his temper with him.

Red Baron pizza stocker goes viral for going off on kid trying to prank him for a social media video. $400k has now been raised for him online & Red Baron says he’s employee of the month. Crazy this “pranker” uploaded this video at all. https://t.co/DYjhP4JvlQ

— Clay Travis (@ClayTravis) October 30, 2026

The wannabe influencer approaches the Red Baron worker, known only as Walt Z, as he was stacking shelves in a grocery store. In the video, he asks for the time, and when Walt gets out his phone, he plays the Apple Pay sound and says, “thanks for the $10.”

It appears the prankster is filming on Meta Ray-Bans and has at least one accomplice recording nearby.

Walt initially walks away, but the prankster comes back for more and says, “Thank you for the $20.” This causes Walt to snap.

“I don’t have time for this cr*p in my life; my life is hard enough as it is,” says Walt, visibly upset. “I got kids at home who went to bed without me… and I was supposed to be at home an hour ago, and I’m still out here working.”

“I’ve got to get up at five in the morning and do 10 more stores,” Walt continues. “So this is not a joke to me.”

“You’re probably making money off this, and I can’t even pay my goddamn mortgage,” Walt adds. “And I’ve got four kids at home.”

It’s a powerful tirade that caught many people’s attention. Fortune reports that the GoFundMe page was set up by a man called Luke Oakenshield who has never met Walt but wanted to track him down after watching the video, which was filmed back in August but only published recently.

The campaign raised $100,000 in three days, with former Republican presidential hopeful Vivek Ramaswamy donating $38,000. After it came close to $800,000, Walt posted a message on GoFundMe thanking people for their support and announcing that he is pausing donations and urging people to support other families in need. He adds that he won’t be giving any interviews.

Call them rage-bait pranks or clout-chasing harassment; these types of videos are a modern phenomenon that has its roots firmly in photography.

Ever since cameras became small enough to take candid photos of unsuspecting people in public places, there has been a conversation about people’s rights in public, exemplified by the influential Harvard Law Review article by Samuel Warren and Louis Brandeis titled The Right to Privacy, published in 1890.

Meta’s smart glasses, which were apparently used to film the video of Walt, are just the latest tool in a long line of clandestine cameras. But with this kind of camera technology being made available to every Tom, Dick, and Harry, it’s becoming clear that society is losing its tolerance for such cruel pranksters.

Sony revives classic Walkman headphones as MDR-3R

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Japanese electronics company Sony has leaned into 1980s nostalgia with its remake of the original Walkman headphones, complete with colourful, interchangeable foam pads.

The MDR-3R, with its slim headband and sliding metal arms, looks almost exactly like the model that debuted with the first portable Walkman cassette player in 1979.

But the design now comes with a USB-C connector that works with most modern devices, feeding into the growing return to wired headphones.

The original MDR-3s pioneered the idea of modern portable headphones and have inspired a host of copycats – both then and now, as retro on-ear headphones have staged a comeback in recent years.

“The value of wired headphones is being rediscovered for their simplicity, ease of use, and freedom from charging or pairing,” Sony said.

The company went back to its archives and stuck close to the lightweight, open-ear design, adding interchangeable blue and orange foam pads and details in recycled plastic.

Sony even replicated the 1979 packaging and reengineered the headphones with the same 23-millimetre driver units to stay true to the original sound – although subtle refinements were apparently made to suit “modern listening preferences”.

On top of the USB-C connector, the product has been updated with a built-in microphone and an inline remote for controlling audio and calls.

Sony has also released a limited-edition version of the MDR-3R with red foam pads and a black headband in collaboration with Netflix‘s Stranger Things, celebrating their “shared 1980s inspiration”.

Retro gadgets have grown increasingly popular in recent years, as Gen Z has rediscovered gadgets such as cassette players, digicams and Tamagotchis.

The trend seems to have grown hand-in-hand with the rise of calm technology, as consumers have grown weary of modern tech.

Barcelona touchline bust-up shows Newcastle United got the better end of the Anthony Gordon deal

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Anthony Gordon isn't having it all his own way at Barcelona if his latest exchange with boss Hansi Flick is anything to go by

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Hansi Flick is a demanding, successful coach who doesn't often lose his cool, but his fury on Sunday evening was difficult to miss.

As Barcelona saw out a hard-fought 2-1 win against a characteristically stubborn Atletico Madrid side, Flick decided to freshen up his attack late on. Anthony Gordon’s number went up on the fourth official's board towards the end of the second half and the Englishman was spared the final few minutes.

Rather than sprinting off, the 25-year-old ambled towards the sideline at a leisurely pace. Flick, visibly enraged, began gesticulating frantically, shouting at the former Newcastle United winger to get off the pitch.

Flick’s anger was justified, though, both in terms of the individual match situation and the rulebook. In LaLiga, the Spanish federation (RFEF) strictly enforces IFAB’s Law 3, requiring substituted players to leave the pitch at the nearest exit point as quickly as possible.

Spanish officials have clamped down hard on dead time this season, meaning dawdling runs the needless risk of a booking for unsporting conduct.

But with Flick keen to see out the result, Gordon’s stroll ran the risk of Barcelona playing a man down at a crucial point in the game. Another of IFAB's rules states that if a player takes longer than 10 seconds to leave the field, the incoming substitute must wait.

Gordon did not take kindly to his manager's frustration, cutting a frustrated figure once he had eventually left the pitch.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

The exchange captured the mood surrounding his start to life in Spain. Initial excitement has worn off, and been replaced by growing scepticism among supporters.

Catalan dailies Sport and Mundo Deportivo have both published critical assessments in recent weeks, openly questioning whether the former Everton man has the technical subtlety required for Spanish football. The recurring criticism in the local media is that Gordon is overly reliant on straight-line pace, struggling for solutions when confronted by opponents who sit deep and deny him space behind.

Supporters on social media have been similarly blunt. Compared to Lamine Yamal and Raphinha who regularly create danger in tight pockets, Gordon has on occasion looked isolated out wide, leading many Culers to question why the club spent heavily on an attacker whose skillset seems at odds with Barcelona's possession-heavy approach.

This will come with a sense of deja vu for Newcastle fans, who will recall the manner in which Gordon departed St James’ Park last season.

After his head was turned by interest from abroad in the spring, his sharpness and overall impact visibly dipped during the run-in. With his desire to leave made clear to the hierarchy, Eddie Howe ultimately took him out of the firing line, leaving him on the bench for the final weeks of the season. Newcastle and their supporters largely accepted the inevitability of the player's exit, and recouping £70 million for Gordon means there is an argument that the Tyneside club got the better end of the deal.

At Newcastle, Gordon had a tactical structure built around his relentless vertical running and pressing triggers, but at Spotify Camp Nou, the blueprint isn't being tailored to suit him. While Sunday’s flare-up with Flick was probably little more than a storm in a tea cup, it is a warning. Even though he has three goals and four assists in 15 outings for Barcelona so far, there is already a feeling - or perhaps even expectation - that he should be doing more.

Ryan Serhant’s warning to brokers: Stop protecting the business you used to have

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Speaking at NAR NXT 2026, Ryan Serhant argued that brokerages should flip their model, deliver leads and support first, then recruit. He said AI should increase production and that office costs must be justified by revenue.  AI Summary

When Ryan Serhant, founder and CEO of SERHANT., launched his brokerage six years ago, he wasn’t interested in following the traditional brokerage playbook. In fact, he believed many of the industry’s largest firms were too focused on protecting their existing businesses rather than preparing for the future.

“The thing that scared me the most was they were very, very focused on protecting the business that they had instead of building for where they’re going,” Serhant said during a conversation at the National Association of Realtors‘ (NAR) 2026 NXT conference.

Now, as his firm expands across the country, Serhant, whose brokerage is RealTrends Verified, is betting on AI, lead generation and a lean operating model to drive growth. He shared what he believes brokers are getting wrong about everything from recruiting and office space to technology investments and what they should be doing instead.

Here are seven takeaways from the conversation.

Serhant says that the script should be flipped when it comes to how brokers think about agents. Rather than ask what agents can contribute to the brokerage, they instead should focus on what the brokerage can deliver to its agents.

For SERHANT., that means lead generation, brand awareness and an operating structure designed to remain efficient as the business grows. “It’s like I want to build a company for me, the way I would want to be led, my number one job is to generate leads.”

He compared the brokerage’s role to that of a power plant, providing the infrastructure agents need to grow their businesses. “The brokerage’s business is to provide electricity. For me, SERHANT. is a power plant, and we provide electricity to our agents and to our leaders in every market that we’re in.”

That value proposition, he said, must be compelling enough to help agents reach higher price points, increase transaction volume and attract customers. “Agents want leads more than they care about split,” Serhant said.

While brokerages are investing in AI tools, Serhant questioned whether most of those investments will generate meaningful returns. “But they won’t. I mean, most of them won’t, to be honest, a tool is only as good as the person who will use it.”

Rather than introduce another technology platform for agents to learn, SERHANT. initially deployed its AI product, S.mple, to support staff to automate tasks and free agents to focus on sales and relationships.

The objective was not simply to save time. It was to increase transactions per agent and improve the brokerage’s operating margins. “And what we saw was the agents who were using S.mple grew their production by 44% on average in one year. But the super agents, the ones who used it and broke it every day, doubled production in the first year in a declining market.”

Serhant said the next evolution involves using AI to handle increasingly complex workflows with less human intervention. For brokers evaluating AI investments, his approach raises a question: Are those tools actually increasing production, or are they simply adding another expense?

Serhant doesn’t believe the growing availability of AI will eliminate the importance of real estate agents. Instead, he sees an opportunity for agents to spend more time on what technology cannot easily replicate.

“If intelligence is cheap, trust is expensive. Trust is now a luxury item.” His strategy is to use AI to take administrative work off agents’ plates, allowing them to focus on building customer relationships.

“How can I develop more trust with more agents, and how can the agents develop more trust with more customers by buying back their time?”

For brokerage leaders, the distinction is important. Technology may make agents more productive, but Serhant believes trust remains a competitive advantage.

SERHANT. initially built its business without physical brokerage offices, choosing instead to invest in lead generation and technology. As the company expanded, Serhant said it established guidelines for when opening an office would make financial sense, including reaching $1 billion in annual transactions.

“Anyone who says they need an office is clinging to a business that used to be, and I don’t want to work with them,” he said. His point is that brokerage expenses must be tied to revenue and productivity rather than traditional expectations of what a real estate company should look like.

Serhant said one of his priorities is to “create the leanest, meanest efficiency model, so that the support, the expense, doesn’t outrun revenue in any market we happen to find ourselves in.”

As SERHANT. expands into new markets, Serhant said the company recruits founding agents who help establish its presence and attract additional agents. He said the brokerage does not rely on financial incentives to persuade agents to join.

“We don’t pay anybody. They all come to us organically. Every agent who’s come to us leaves money on the table because they come for the opportunity and they stay for the results,” he said.

Serhant argued that agents recruited primarily through financial incentives are likely to remain focused on those incentives. “If they come for the money, they stay for the money. Just like a bad marriage, right?”

His preferred approach is to build a brokerage with a recognizable brand, lead opportunities and tools that enable agents to produce more than they could elsewhere.

Asked how he maintains SERHANT.’s culture as the company expands, Serhant offered one an observations about leadership. “I define culture as the behavior you allow. It’s not policy guidelines. It’s not the employee handbook. It’s [not] the people you bring in, but culture is defined by the behavior you allow to exist, not the behavior you encourage.”

Serhant acknowledged that he made mistakes early in the company’s growth by allowing high-producing individuals to remain despite behavior that negatively affected the organization. “It’s the hardest thing to do. Ah, this person’s so good at what they do, but they just suck at it as a human. It is always so much better to chop out the poison and let the rest rise to the top.”

For brokers facing pressure to retain productive agents, his message was that short-term production should not come at the expense of the company’s culture.

During the audience Q&A, a small brokerage owner asked Serhant how firms with fewer than 25 agents should approach a changing housing market. His advice was to stop relying on the business strategies that worked under different conditions. “Sometimes there are markets that reward us. Sometimes there are markets that punish us.”

Serhant suggested that brokers look for opportunities in other segments, including multifamily, leasing and commercial real estate, when their traditional sources of transactions decline. “We get frustrated because we get stuck trying to do things that used to work instead of being a little uncomfortable and pushing forward to do the things that could work.”

The message applies beyond small brokerages. Throughout the conversation, Serhant returned to the importance of challenging established business models, keeping expenses in check and building a company that can adapt. For brokerage leaders, the takeaway is that surviving a difficult market may require more than waiting for sales to rebound. It may mean reconsidering how their companies generate business, support agents and define success.

Dave Challinor's private Sheffield Wednesday notes exposed during Oldham defeat

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Dave Challinor's debut as Sheffield Wednesday manager didn't go to plan, as the Owls were dumped out of the FA Cup by League Two opposition in Oldham Athletic, and there were parts of the performance he was especially angered by.

Oldham were good value for their win after extra time, with Wednesday putting in a tired performance that not many were expecting from Challinor's first game in charge. It's the first time that the 51-year-old has opened up a dugout role without a win.

The first half, especially, wasn't good enough by the new manager's standards. Wednesday dominated possession, but only had one shot and just three touches in the Latics' box. It was quite reminiscent of what angered Owls fans about Henrik Pedersen's setup.

Challinor wasn't going to let this become the norm, though, and was seen writing plenty of notes in his notebook for what was going to be quite the angry team talk at the break.

Oldham's win over Wednesday was shown live on TNT Sports on Sunday evening, and with the TV cameras out, the Owls were unable to put on a show in the first half of Dave Challinor's reign.

Arrow keys or WASD to move. P to pause, M to mute.

Swipe on the pitch or use the pad to move.

The cameras caught an over-the-shoulder shot of the Wednesday boss making some notes midway through the first half, and he'd already had a full page's worth of grievances.

Amongst some more expletive expressions to describe parts of that first half, there were the words "WEAK," "compete !!" and "Stupid !"

Immediately, Wednesday fans took to X to try and see if anyone could make out what was being written by their new boss midway through the first half, which led to many screenshots, identical to the one above, being tweeted.

"Not a happy boy. Quite rightly," one Wednesday fan said in response to those notes being relayed on X during the game. "Hahahahhahaa that’s class. He’s impressed I see," another commented.

"This is hilarious btw. It took him 2 minutes to see how dire Palmer is," was also among the comments, as was "That result might just be what Challinor needed to witness."

Sheffield Wednesday fans soured very quickly on Henrik Pedersen when results weren't going their way, but despite bowing out of the FA Cup at the first hurdle to a side a division below, the majority of reaction during and after the game was directed at the performance on the pitch, rather than in the dugout.

After the game, Challinor continued to cut a frustrated figure, with the majority of fans agreeing with what he was saying, with the overarching feeling that some of the players aren't where they should be physically at this stage in the campaign.

Do you agree with Dave Challinor's assessment of his first game as Sheffield Wednesday boss? Let us know in the comments.

Most Wednesday supporters felt disappointed with how Pedersen would protect players after poor results, whereas the new boss seems content with outlining where things aren't good enough, which in turn is winning fans over.

While it would have been an ideal scenario for Dave Challinor to walk in, tweak one or two things and Wednesday go from strength to strength, the first 45 minutes of his tenure in charge highlight that this definitely won't be the case.

Challinor is coming into a side that, by his own admission, isn't there physically and is struggling massively with injury.

Mason Burstow is the latest casualty, while Ashley Maynard-Brewer was forced off during the game and Harry Gray was removed from the squad at the last minute after failing to get through the warm-up.

Football League World assesses and ranks the FIFTEEN best away fanbases in the EFL right now

The 51-year-old, evidently, is going to need time, but it feels that the Wednesday fanbase, or at least the most vocal parts, are happy to give him that. At least, more so than they were with Challinor's predecessor.

To many, Challinor was the number one option to take Wednesday forward at the start of the season, and now he's in post; those in the stands at Hillsborough are willing to give him enough time to take them forward. In the meantime, his ruthless nature is helping them get onside, even if the results take some time to follow suit.

Coframe Raises $25M Series A, Bringing Total Funding to $35M

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Coframe announced on November 9, 2026 that it had raised $35 million in total funding, including a $25 million Series A co-led by Khosla Ventures and Hanabi Capital, according to its funding release.

Leaders at OpenAI, Anthropic and Google DeepMind also participated in the round, while Nat Friedman and Antler are listed among Coframe’s broader backers. Josh Payne founded the company in 2023. Coframe is developing websites, landing pages and advertisements that generate, test and improve variations of themselves rather than remaining static between redesigns.

Payne calls the underlying vision the living internet. “A digital experience should understand what it is trying to accomplish, adapt to the person interacting with it and learn from every result,” he said. The approach shifts website work from periodic redesigns toward ongoing experiments whose results inform subsequent changes.

The system assigns AI agents to strategy, design, software engineering, quality assurance and data analysis. Coframe says they can ideate, build, test and optimize hundreds of website and advertising variations in minutes. Its proprietary user-behavior model is trained on interactions across hundreds of millions of sessions each month, allowing results from one experiment to inform later ones.

Coframe’s product overview describes a workflow that scores and ranks proposed experiments by expected conversion and revenue impact. The system then creates on-brand interfaces, writes code and runs automated quality assurance across devices. Customers approve variations before they go live. Installation uses a JavaScript snippet, and Coframe can operate alongside existing testing tools.

Coframe reports more than $300 million in incremental revenue generated for customers and an average conversion-rate improvement above 20% across a customer base that includes Dropbox, BCG, Block, Backcountry and Sur La Table. The revenue figure measures customer gains, not Coframe’s own sales.

Coframe reports a 158% conversion lift for Dropbox over six months and a 39% experiment win rate across thousands of variants. At Dropbox, an experiment that previously required four weeks of engineering, design and quality-assurance work was built and deployed in 30 minutes. Together, the figures present the company’s case that automating production and analysis can increase both the pace and the outcome of experimentation.

Coframe says it is also working with OpenAI on the ChatGPT ads offering, aiming to let customers optimize an advertisement and its landing page as one continuously learning experience. The work builds on an earlier collaboration to train a specialized model for generating on-brand code and creative assets.

OpenAI’s October 1, 2024 vision fine-tuning announcement provides an example of that earlier work. OpenAI reported that Coframe fine-tuned GPT-4o with images and code, producing a 26% improvement in generating websites with consistent visual style and correct layout compared with base GPT-4o. That project focused on generating digital content; Coframe’s newly described user-behavior model instead learns from interactions across digital experiences.

Coframe is also preparing for AI agents that research, compare and buy on people’s behalf. It says agent best practices are being incorporated into every website it builds, while its product overview describes agent-facing pages designed for AI systems to read, understand and cite. Apps, email and other digital experiences remain roadmap items rather than capabilities announced as generally available.

The central challenge is extending the reported speed and conversion gains from website experiments to advertising and agent-readable pages, while preserving the customer approval controls that govern what goes live.

A River Runs Through It. An AI Data Center Tried to Move In.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Anglers enjoy the iconic Blackfoot River in Montana, known for its blue ribbon trout fishing (Photo: Matt Roberts)

Brenna Love grew up fishing on the Blackfoot, a snow-fed Montana mountain river that spans 130 miles from its headwaters on the Continental Divide to the confluence of the Clark Fork River, seven miles east of Missoula. Its cold, fast-flowing water makes for premier blue ribbon trout fishing, scenic canyons, and whitewater recreation.

The Blackfoot was immortalized in Norman Maclean’s 1976 book A River Runs Through It and Other Stories, which was turned into a Hollywood film starring Brad Pitt in 1992.

Love, 29, is a resident of Bonner, a former mill town with just 2,700 residents located along the banks of the Blackfoot.

“My earliest memories are on my family’s raft,” Love told Outside. “Every time you’re on the water, you’re surrounded by other people loving the same backyard that we love so deeply. You look around and see people on boats of all shapes, sizes, and colors, recreating and enjoying. It’s the reason we live here in Montana.”

In March of 2026, an Idaho-based technology company called Krambu announced plans to build an artificial intelligence data center in Bonner, just steps from the banks of the Blackfoot. The project would convert an old mill building into a warehouse for computer servers, and the complex would draw water from the nearby Blackfoot River to provide continuous cooling for the computer racks.

Love and her community fought back—and won.

“It was hard,” she told Outside. “It was really hard. It took organizing and talking with each other about what this means. It took our evenings, our lunch breaks, reviewing the new application materials and doing our own research.”

It’s the latest in a growing wave of community pushback against big tech’s data center expansion—and a reminder that local voices, when organized, can have a lasting impact.

Data centers are rapidly popping up across the United States, fueled by the computing needs of artificial intelligence and cloud-based software. These centers house massive servers, data storage systems, and networking equipment, and they require sizable amounts of electricity and water to run and maintain the hardware.

According to Public Lands Intelligence, as of this writing, 1,302 data centers are proposed or under construction, in addition to 904 already operating across 49 states and Washington, D.C.

But as construction crews have erected these centers, they have become political lightning rods in towns and cities. The centers are often loud and large, and they eat up valuable electricity. As data centers have faced bipartisan backlash in cities across the country, tech firms have looked to rural communities, farming towns, and even unused public land to build.

At least 35 of current data center applications are on public lands, the Center for Western Priorities reports, and some are directly adjacent to national park units.

Secretary of the Department of the Interior Doug Burgum has tens of millions invested in AI data centers, according to a report published by ProPublica. In September, The Washington Sun reported that the Trump administration was considering at least 12 data center-related infrastructure projects on federal public lands.

Private equity firm Blackstone is building the Tallgrass Energy Project, a massive AI data center located south of Cheyenne, Wyoming. Blackstone also backed a historic $105 million donation to Yellowstone National Park.

The list goes on. Roughly 115 miles from Yellowstone’s north entrance, Quantica’s Big Sky Campus is proposing a 5,100-acre, or nearly eight-square-mile, data center. In Kentucky, Cave Point Commerce Center is a proposed 381-acre hyperscale data center near Mammoth Cave National Park. The City of Page, Arizona, is considering a $10 billion data center two miles from Horseshoe Bend near Glen Canyon National Recreation Area. In July, the federal government fast-tracked a data center ten miles from Lake Mead.

In Bonner, the proposed data center would be in the Bonner Mill, a historic 169-acre former lumber mill along the riverbank. Originally built in 1886, the sawmill closed in 2008 and was transformed in 2011 into a multi-tenant industrial park.

Ben Catton, a legislative assistant at the Montana Environmental Information Center (MEIC), told Outside that sites like the Bonner Mill are ideal for companies that build data centers.

“Tech companies target old mill sites,” Catton said. “That’s part of their business model: to look for this existing infrastructure. Because those were really energy-consumptive sites, they have a lot of that energy infrastructure in place already.”

According to the Krambu proposal, the Bonner Mill data center would draw 16 million gallons of water from the river in July and August to cool its servers. Under Catton’s calculations, that means the 240,000-square-foot center would require roughly one megawatt of power—enough to power 800 homes for a year.

Montana’s energy and water needs during its driest months didn’t sit well with him.

“They’re going to be very consumptive in the two months when the landscape is the thirstiest for that water, when our rivers are at their lowest, and when our fish are most in need of cold water,” Catton said.

The Bonner Mill sits 100 yards from Love’s front porch, and as a concerned community member, she began reaching out to her neighbors one by one to discuss the plan.

“Start by having a conversation with your neighbor, and then your other neighbor, and then your other neighbor,” Love said. “Those conversations really build up to a community-wide conversation.”

On March 20, local nonprofit Friends of Two Rivers—so-named for the confluence of the Clark Fork and Blackfoot Rivers—hosted the first public meeting at Saint Ann Catholic Church, a rural, single-story neighborhood church originally built in 1905. Against a wood-paneled backdrop, representatives from Krambu and Missoula County, along with Bonner Mill property manager Mike Heisey, spoke on a panel to a standing-room-only crowd.

Organizers were surprised by how many people showed up, Love recalled. Most people were there to voice their concerns about the project.

“There were just so many different people engaging in so many different ways through different avenues, and that’s what eventually changed the tide,” Love said. “It was organized in all of these different pockets, and I think any one of those groups couldn’t have done it alone.”

It was the first of many meetings held at libraries, schools, in living rooms, and at coffee shops. Love said she participated in at least eight, primarily working with her HOA members in closed meetings, as well as informal working groups of neighbors and the managers of the Bonner Milltown Historical Center.

“Rather than a scattershot signs and flyers approach, we were trying to find avenues where we thought we could make an impact,” Love said. “We organized as our HOA and then submitted our concerns and questions directly to the property manager.”

Love is one of hundreds who rallied through social media and in person to oppose the data center. Local regulations at the time weren’t rigid enough to deny Krambu approval, so Love and her team took their concerns directly to the property manager.

“The more direct action and impact you can make, the better,” Love said. “If you can figure out who has the authority, whether it is your county or your city, via the regulations in place there, the owner of the site, or who has investment in the community, find them.”

Between March and June of 2026, Krambu submitted five iterations of its application to build the data center, each time drawing backlash from the community. Love and other local advocates launched a petition that garnered more than 50,000 signatories to reject the AI data center, and flooded every town meeting with shoulder-to-shoulder opposition.

Citing overwhelming public feedback and neighborhood opposition, Heisey officially announced on July 6, 2026, that the property would not move forward with the data center and withdrew his support for the application.

A Krambu spokesperson told Outside in a statement that the company is under new leadership and that the Bonner data center was spearheaded by the company’s former management. The company has no plans to revisit the Bonner project, the spokesperson said.

In direct response to the community concerns exposed during the Krambu review, Missoula County Commissioners approved a temporary one-year moratorium on July 9 on new zoning permits for large-scale data centers to establish more comprehensive regulatory frameworks.

Love credits the victory to her community’s ability to organize across multiple fronts: viral online opposition, petitions, public comments and meeting attendance, coordinated feedback from small groups and the HOA, and direct outreach to decision-makers, from county commissioners and city council members to the landowner.

It’s a big win for a small mountain town, and one that could pave the way for other rural areas facing data center proposals.

Montana is perhaps uniquely positioned for these types of fights. The Montana Constitution enshrines the right to a “clean and healthful environment” as a fundamental right, requiring the state and individuals to maintain and improve the environment for present and future generations. Outdoor recreation is also a major economic input for Montana, contributing $3.8 billion to the Montana economy.

Adam Schellhammer, Mid-Atlantic regional director for the nonprofit advocacy group American Rivers, warns that data centers pose new threats to states without such incentives or protections.

“On the national level, trends show that the speed and scale of development is just ramping up so quickly that we’re just having trouble keeping up,” Schellhammer said.

According to Schellhammer, data center regulation is fragmented across federal, state, and local municipal agencies. Water usage details are frequently concealed under non-disclosure agreements or classified as proprietary corporate information. Because many areas do not legally mandate public disclosure, city planners cannot always establish accurate water budgets to understand a center’s full impact. Schellhammer says communities should start by updating industrial zoning and land-use ordinances to make a difference, rather than reacting after sites are chosen.

“The lesson learned is that every single voice matters,” Love said. “If there’s something you care about, say so. Find a place to speak your truth, speak your concerns, be an advocate for the things that you are passionate about, and someone will hear you.”
