Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Nvidia Board Investigates Jensen Huang After Report He Was Target of Chinese Spy Effort

Nvidia's board of directors has opened an independent investigation following reports that CEO Jensen Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort, the company said on Sunday.

A committee of independent directors, assisted by outside counsel, will oversee the inquiry. "While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," it said, adding that it "has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team."

We just issued a press release from our board of directors. Read the release: https://t.co/W9yXWJzbkk

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 8, 2026

The investigation follows a Financial Times report on Saturday that a U.S. government memo, written earlier this year based on intelligence information, said the Nvidia CEO had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed, citing people familiar with the memo. U.S. intelligence believes she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security, the FT reported. Nvidia declined to comment to the newspaper on the memo. Huang's email to staff, sent on Sunday, did not name the person with whom he had an affair.

Lawmakers from both parties are seeking answers. Republican leaders have asked Huang to appear privately before the House China, foreign affairs and intelligence committees, according to the FT, while Sen. Chris Coons (D-DE), who will chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee from January, pledged in a tweet that "Congress will investigate and get answers."

The FT's report built on a New York Times investigation last month, which said U.S. intelligence agencies had concluded that Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, a Chinese citizen, was a Chinese intelligence asset, and that surveillance of her had revealed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. President Donald Trump was briefed on the findings roughly a year ago and chose not to act on them, according to the Times.

U.S. officials suspected Megaspeed of helping Chinese companies get around restrictions on advanced AI chips, and the Commerce Department's investigation into the company has been handed to the Justice Department, the Times reported. Megaspeed's sister company in Malaysia bought at least $4.8 billion worth of AI equipment containing Nvidia chips between June 2024 and February 2026, according to trade records cited by the newspaper.

Nvidia told the Times that Huang's interactions with Ms. Huang were "not unusual or unexpected," and that customers like Megaspeed receive "ordinary business information." Megaspeed said it had retained outside counsel to conduct an internal investigation, Ms. Huang did not respond to the newspaper's requests for comment, and China's embassy in Washington dismissed the allegations as "groundless smears and slanders."

In an email to employees on Sunday, Huang apologized to his family and colleagues. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," he wrote, according to a copy published by CNBC.

"Regarding the national security concerns raised in news reports, NVIDIA's Board of Directors has initiated an independent investigation. I support the investigation and will cooperate fully," Huang wrote. "I am committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security." He asked for privacy for his family.

"Today's news will not slow NVIDIA. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead NVIDIA as CEO without interruption," the company told CNBC.

Nvidia shares were down 5% at $177.32 in premarket trading early on Monday, after closing at $186.64 on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance.

Spines Raises $64M Through Series A-1 Equity and Growth Financing

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Spines announced $64 million in growth capital on November 9, 2026, combining a $29 million Series A-1 equity round with a $35 million non-dilutive growth financing facility to accelerate the expansion of its AI publishing platform for authors.

Zeev Ventures led the equity round, with participation from Aleph, NordStar, M-Fund, LionTree and angel investor Dan Senor. Twelve provided the separate facility. Aleph previously led Spines’ seed round and participated in the Series A before returning for the Series A-1.

Spines reports reaching a $50 million revenue run rate and 400% year-over-year growth. More than 8,000 American authors have published books through the platform, and around 400 new authors join each month. The growth figures arrive as Spines broadens its tools from book production into marketing, translation and audio.

Authors arrive with their own manuscripts and use Spines to manage the publishing process. Specialized AI agents handle editing, proofreading, cover design, formatting, metadata, translation, audiobook production and marketing tasks. Authors can also work with Spines’ in-house human editors, proofreaders, designers and native-speaking language professionals when they want additional human support.

That model is deliberately different from using AI to draft the book. “We don’t believe the future of publishing is AI writing books. We believe the future is AI giving authors superpowers,” said founder and CEO Yehuda Niv.

Spines says its workflow shortens a publishing process that traditionally took 12 to 18 months to a matter of weeks, while its distribution network spans more than 100 global channels. Sales tracking and royalty information feed into a centralized management system, giving authors visibility into earnings across platforms and markets.

Spines’ campaign-management tools let authors create, launch and manage marketing campaigns intended to reach relevant readers. That puts audience development inside the same system used to prepare and distribute a title, extending the platform’s role beyond production.

For audiobooks, authors can use synthetic voices or create a model of their own voice from a short recording, then use that model for narration in other languages. A separate illustration tool for children’s books is designed to keep characters consistent throughout a book, allowing illustrated titles to remain within the same publishing workflow.

Spines says it currently supports AI translation from English into Spanish, Italian, French, German, Portuguese, Polish and Hebrew, followed by native-speaking human review, and plans to expand from those seven target languages to 20 over the next six months.

Oren Zeev, founding partner of Zeev Ventures and an early Audible investor, said he believes Spines can become a global platform for authors to publish and reach readers, citing a close friend’s positive experience publishing a book through the service.

Aleph co-founder and general partner Michael Eisenberg, the author of nine books, said he backed Spines because technology can reduce the slow, fragmented work that follows a manuscript without removing the author from the process.

The financing supports a service that covers both book preparation and the infrastructure that helps a title reach readers. The open question for Spines’ next phase is whether its reported author growth and planned language expansion can support a larger international publishing business while preserving the author-led model.

Pierre Sage vit très probablement ses dernières heures sur le banc de Crystal Palace en ce début de trêve internationale de novembre 2026. Arrivé l'été dernier, le technicien français est sur le point d'être démis de ses fonctions après un début de saison catastrophique en Premier League.

Dimanche 8 novembre 2026, Crystal Palace a concédé un nouveau revers à domicile lors de la 10e journée de Premier League en s'inclinant face à Liverpool (1-3) à Selhurst Park. Menés après des buts signés Alexis Mac Allister (40e), Florian Wirtz (54e) et Alexander Isak (89e), les Londoniens ont réagi trop tard grâce à un penalty transformé par Eddie Nketiah (90e+5). Cette défaite face aux Reds constitue le septième revers en dix journées de championnat pour Crystal Palace, qui n'a remporté que deux matches depuis le début de saison. Une situation critique. Le club londonien glisse ainsi à la 20e et dernière place du classement et se retrouve lanterne rouge pour la première fois depuis près de neuf ans, en décembre 2017.

La direction de Crystal Palace aurait déjà acté la décision de limoger Pierre Sage... et cela avant même la rencontre de dimanche soir. Interrogé sur son avenir, l'entraîneur français a livré son analyse en conférence de presse d'après-match :

Pierre Sage avait signé à Crystal Palace à la mi-juin 2026 pour un contrat de trois ans en provenance du RC Lens afin de remplacer Oliver Glasner. Le bilan global du technicien français à la tête des Eagles s'avère toutefois bien insuffisant, avec 5 victoires, 3 nuls et 8 défaites en 16 matches disputés toutes compétitions confondues. Toutefois, d'autres profils sont également cités par Sky Sports pour s'installer sur le banc de Crystal Palace. Parmi les pistes envisagées figurent notamment Frank Lampard, Sean Dyche, Vitor Pereira, Kim Hellberg, Vitor Matos et Tony Popovic. Le calendrier impose en tout cas une réaction rapide pour le club de Selhurst Park, dont le prochain match en Premier League est fixé au samedi 21 novembre 2026 sur le terrain de Coventry City.

The Latest Shift in How Appraisals Are Handled Could Save Homebuyers Hundreds in Closing Costs—Eventually

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

It’s typically the last hurdle for homebuyers—and the process is about to get a face-lift.

Last week, the Uniform Appraisal Dataset (UAD) 3.6, a major update to property valuation standards introduced by Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac, became mandatory for all new UCDP appraisal report submissions.

UAD 3.6 is a new, digital form that appraisers will use to evaluate homes. Now, rather than the appraiser meticulously jotting down notes in longform paragraphs, the new system will allow appraisers to use extensive drop-down menus and checkboxes to make their evaluations.

A proper appraisal can determine not only whether a buyer can qualify for a loan, but also whether they can save significant money at closing.

After a buyer makes an offer, their mortgage lender will order an independent appraisal to confirm the home is worth at least what's being borrowed against it.

If the appraised value comes in at or above the purchase price, the process moves forward as planned. But if it comes in low, that gap has to be resolved before the loan can close.

The logic behind the UAD 3.6 update, according to Fannie and Freddie, is to create a “data-driven, dynamic reporting structure to capture property and market information.”

This new form includes significantly more data fields, helping lenders and buyers better understand the property’s characteristics.

But with more detail comes a longer commitment.

“Be prepared for a lot more questions from the appraiser during the inspection,” explains Michael Mignogna, president of the Appraisal Institute.

“There’s a lot more data to include, so be prepared to answer questions about renovations, repairs, new systems like heating and air conditioning, appliances, etc., or have that information ready if no one with that type of information can be at the inspection.”

To that end, with UAD 3.6, appraisers will now not only grade individual rooms, but they can also highlight upgrades and features that might’ve been lost in the old system. So, features of a newly remodeled kitchen or new bathroom flooring get specifically noted, which will aid in the ease of deciding the home’s value.

Still, Mignogna is skeptical that this will have much of an impact on the final value.

“Not as much information is always known about the comparables, so expectations about condition/quality adjustments to the comps on a room-by-room basis should be tempered,” he adds.

The buyer almost always pays for the appraisal. The cost is typically a few hundred dollars, and it’s often rolled into closing costs. This is the case even though the appraiser is hired by the lender, not the buyer.

The hope is that because this new appraisal process is a clean, digital checklist, banks can review and approve it much faster, reducing delays.

For example, under the former process, lenders had the option to kick back the report for corrections if anything about the report was unclear, which could happen if the appraiser’s report didn’t cover every facet necessary.

When that happens, buyers may find themselves having to pay for expensive mortgage rate-lock extensions to keep their rates while they wait for the appraisal to clear.

Extension fees typically range from 0.125% to 0.250% (or up to 0.500% or more) of the buyer's total loan balance per extension period, often broken into 15-day increments.

On a $450,000 mortgage, a 15-day extension would usually cost between $500 and $1,000.

Given that closing costs already run a buyer anywhere from 2% to 5% of the total loan amount, or about $8,000 to $20,000 for a $400,000 loan, according to Bankrate, another couple hundred to a thousand dollars is nothing to scoff at.

But as the saying goes, all good things come with time, and right now, this new digital infrastructure may actually be coming at a cost to buyers as it finds its footing.

Clear Capital, a real estate valuation technology company, reported via National Mortgage Professional an average $65 increase in appraisal fees in markets where it has enough production volume to compare UAD 3.6 with the older UAD 2.6 format.

Additionally, Michael Farrell, president of the National Association of Mortgage Brokers, said that the number of field appraisers equipped to do UAD 3.6 is so limited at this point, demand bottlenecks are driving up prices for modernized reports. While older appraisals are being priced at around $550, modernized reports may cost borrowers closer to $850.

“It’s basically all roads lead to chaos,” Farrell explained to Scotsman Guide.

“From an originator standpoint, it's a delay. Delays cost money. Delays cost the consumer money.”

Mignogna, meanwhile, says it’s important to remember that fees are affected by far more than just the process.

“Appraisal fees are very specific to markets and are affected by the number of appraisers in a market, the demand for appraisals in that market, interest rates, and a host of other factors,” he says.

Having said that, the transition to the UAD 3.6 format continues to be slow and Mignogna concedes that lenders may bid up prices for the few field appraisers who are fully onboarded like in any “supply-demand situation.”

But while delays are happening as appraisers go through a “transition mode,” Mignogna says this won’t affect buyers' need for expensive mortgage rate-lock extensions.

“This is really only meaningful or significant in times of interest rate uncertainty or expected rate increases,” he says.

“The overall UAD 3.6 process is longer right now for the reasons above, but as the learning curve flattens, turnaround times will improve. It remains to be seen if the increased data requirements offset by UAD 3.6 familiarity gets us to the turnaround times seen before UAD 3.6 or faster turn times.”

With that in mind, it’s a good idea to ask your loan officer upfront if their specific appraisal management company has fully integrated the new digital system into their team.

While the UAD 3.6 mandate took effect on Nov. 2, 2026,  lenders may continue to submit revisions for existing UAD 2.6 reports through May 19, 2027.

Her work has also appeared on MSN, Yahoo News, and BlogHer. She is a proud graduate of Emerson College in Boston and is originally from New Jersey.

inside mikoü architecture and its search for meaning in craft and construction

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

from the intimacy of the traditional riad to the engineering of contemporary buildings, the french-moroccan twin architects explore how cultural memory, craftsmanship, and industrialization can inform a more holistic approach to architecture.

In We Are Building Ourselves, a film by Louisiana Channel, Salwa and Selma Mikoü describe architecture as a process that extends beyond the production of buildings. ‘As architects, we are building also our own personality, our own culture, our own way to see the world. And project after project, we push boundaries that could be also our own boundaries as women,’ the architects share. For the Paris-based twin sisters, every project is a way to establish their position in the world and negotiate the cultural, professional, and personal experiences that shape their work.

That idea is reflected in a practice that brings together seemingly opposing forms of knowledge. The founders of Mikoü Architecture draw on Moroccan traditions of craft and ornament while exploring prefabrication, industrialization, and the technical logic of construction. They are interested in how engineering can become part of architectural expression, how industrial processes can coexist with craftsmanship, and how cultural memory can inform buildings without becoming a superficial visual reference.

In the Louisiana Channel film, the sisters discuss architecture through the language of making: the assembly of materials, the relationship between structure and ornament, the social qualities of space, and the knowledge carried across generations.

Before establishing Mikoü Architecture in Paris in 2008, the sisters each worked at Ateliers Jean Nouvel and Renzo Piano Building Workshop. These experiences exposed them to different approaches to architectural thinking and making, from the development of a strong conceptual position to the practical knowledge involved in realizing a building. For the sisters, engineering is the skeleton of a building, an underlying system that can inform its spatial and visual character. Their interest in prefabrication and industrialization comes from this understanding of construction as an integral part of design. They describe industrialization as a ‘progress gesture’, while acknowledging that these methods are ‘still not standard at all’.

The architects bring craftsmanship and industrial production into the same conversation. They are interested in prefabrication and what it makes possible, but also in the knowledge that comes from working with materials and understanding how things are made. One does not have to come at the expense of the other.

They also speak about ornament in relation to structure and how it can occur from the construction process itself. In the film, they discuss the tension between what is exposed and what is hidden, comparing it to archaeology and ruins, where the remains of a structure offer clues to what once existed.

Born in Fez, Morocco, Salwa and Selma Mikoü draw on their relationship with the country as part of their thinking. They speak about Marrakech as an ‘immersive city’ and about the strong impressions that places leave on them. These experiences shape how they think about space, particularly the relationship between private life and the outside world. They point to the traditional riad, with its terraces and inward-facing spaces, where neighbors can see and speak to one another.

Speaking about Art de Rives, their project in Bordeaux, they describe it as ‘a kind of urban millefeuille’, combining housing, artist residencies, and student accommodation. Its relationship with the Garonne is central to the project, which seeks to open the development toward the river and the surrounding city. The architects’ experience of growing up in Morocco and building their careers in France brings two different architectural cultures into their work. Moroccan craft traditions, particularly the use of ornament, remain an important reference, while their years working within the French architectural scene have shaped their understanding of contemporary practice.

Working together has shaped the way Salwa and Selma Mikoü think and work. They describe a process that involves a lot of talking and drawing, with collage becoming one of the tools they return to when developing ideas. They call it part of their DNA. Being twins also means that their professional and personal lives are closely connected, something they reflect on in the film when discussing how people often see them as one person.

The sisters speak about architecture as a way of shaping who they are, not only as architects but as women finding their place in a profession that has its own expectations and established hierarchies. Every project gives them another opportunity to question those limits.

Luxury Home Prices Are Falling, but These Pockets of Growth Are Defying the Slump

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Luxury home prices are sinking in much of the country, but there are significant exceptions, where high-end home prices just keep rising.

The national threshold for luxury homes—defined as the top 10% of listings—in October came in at $1,197,014, down 2.3% from one year ago, according to the latest Realtor.com® monthly luxury report.

The downward trend held in the upper tiers of the luxury market, with the 95th percentile down 4.1% to a threshold of $1,873,040 and the 99th percentile down 6.2% to $5,125,874.

However, in certain golden pockets, the luxury market is thriving. Of the 43 metros in the report's dataset, 12 posted a year-over-year increase of 1% or higher.

"Although the price point for entry luxury has had 31 consecutive months of annual decline at the national level, there are markets where prices are climbing and, in some cases, well above the year-ago levels," says the report's author, Realtor.com senior economist Anthony Smith.

Of those 12 robust luxury markets, the South massively dominates, with five markets in Florida alone. Only two Northern metros—Atlantic City and Detroit—made the cut.

Nor are these necessarily the most expensive markets in the U.S. While those pricey markets—with familiar names like Bridgeport-Stamford-Danbury, CT, and Los Angeles-Long Beach-Anaheim, CA—have the highest entry thresholds to luxury, their prices are slipping.

Of the 12 most expensive markets, only Naples-Marco Island, FL, with an entry threshold of $3,753,333, continues to show price gains. Nor are these gains negligible—the metro's luxury threshold is up a very healthy 9.0% year over year.

While this is only 1.3 percentage points over second-place finisher North Port-Bradenton-Sarasota, FL, with a much lower entry to luxury at $1,796,850, it is a full 4.3 percentage points over third-place finisher Charleston-North Charleston, SC.

Naples comes ahead in another key way, too. Luxury properties are moving a median 27 days faster than last year, or 21.1% quicker, by far the fastest pace increase of all the growth metros. The second-place finisher, Cape Coral-Fort Myers, FL, is moving 18 days faster than last year, or 16.1%.

The price gains aren't limited to entry-level luxury, or the top 10%. Eight of the 12 metros saw all three luxury tiers rise from a year ago, a sharp contrast with the national picture, where all those thresholds fell.

The largest gain for the top 1% of listings (ultraluxury) came from Charleston-North Charleston, SC. Here, the entry-level luxe threshold rose 4.7% (the third-highest in the nation), but its top 1% of properties blasted past that with a median price increase of 18.7%, for a threshold of $8,044,460.

These are the top 12 growth markets for entry-level luxury:

North Port-Bradenton-Sarasota, FL (up 7.7%)

Miami-Fort Lauderdale-West Palm Beach, FL (up 4.1%)

Nashville-Davidson-Murfreesboro-Franklin, TN (up 3.6%)

Las Vegas-Henderson-North Las Vegas, NV (up 2.2%)

Tampa-St. Petersburg-Clearwater, FL (up 2.1%)

On the southwestern edge of the Sunshine State, dubbed the Paradise Coast, Naples has long lured snowbirds eager to escape Northern winters and embrace the blue-green waters and white sandy shores of the Gulf.

But it hasn't always been a top luxury haven; in fact, that aspect of the area is rather new.

Luxury broker Taylor Canada of Gulf Coast International Properties, who was born and raised in Naples, says that in the 1980s, the town was "nothing."

"On the beach, there were like four buildings," he tells Realtor.com.

But over the decades, the agent has seen the metro change dramatically, with booming development and sprawling Tuscan-style mansions and high-end retail. Still, the town has managed to retain much of its "small town" charm, thanks to tight building regulations.

"They are really trying to make it not Miami," he says of the city. "That attracts a lot of affluent buyers to the lifestyle and the quietness."

The wealthy in Palm Beach County are "more showy," he says. "Here, you could be behind a billionaire in the grocery store and never know."

The broker, who currently represents a $9 million, four-bedroom, 4.5-bath 5,000-plus-square-foot estate in Old Naples, isn't surprised that his hometown is bucking the national trend, with luxury prices on the rise.

"We're seeing records broken monthly," he says of certain exclusive and waterfront neighborhoods. Even the luxury condo market, which he says "struggled for a few years," is showing signs of a recovery, with the occasional bidding war.

Buyers at this level are the usual suspects, hailing from more expensive states such as New York, Washington, and California. But, uniquely, the area also sees a lot of local buyers who "shuffle around" from one luxury property to another.

"Every three to five years, they figure out where they want to go next," Canada says of mobile Naples lovers. "They go, 'Oh, maybe I want to be on the water, or maybe I want to get closer to the beach, or maybe I want to be in a golf community.'"

And while Naples still draws an older crowd, the agent says it is more common now to see buyers "in their 40s and their families." While the techies tend to head to Miami and the West Coast, the CEOs and business leaders prefer the East Coast, he says.

And what they want are properties that are brand-new, turnkey, and furnished.

"They're willing to pay a premium for that," he adds.

Florida's plethora of tax breaks—including the newly approved measure that would reform the state's property tax system—is luring high net worth individuals more than ever before.

"The tax break is paying for their property, almost," he says.

The metro with the second-highest annual growth in the nation, with a 7.7% increase, is 120 miles north of Naples, nestled along Sarasota Bay. Luxe homes, with an entry threshold of $1,796,850, are moving nine days faster than they did last year.

The North Port-Bradenton-Sarasota metro encompasses multiple barrier islands, including Longboat Key and Siesta Key, with famous Siesta Beach, which often tops "beat beaches" polls due to its nearly 100% quartz-crystal sand.

Like Naples, Sarasota wasn't always a luxury haven.

Local broker Joel Schemmel of Douglas Elliman has been representing the area for 25 years and concentrates on the top 1% of properties, or those over $4 million.

Those with fat pocketbooks began pouring into Sarasota during the COVID-19 pandemic, he says. Now, buyers continue to be lured by the pristine white beaches, cultural attractions (Sarasota has a vibrant arts community), and top-notch schools, including the renowned IMG Academy sports prep school in Bradenton.

"We've really seen movement in the ultraluxury market," he tells Realtor.com, noting that sales priced at $4 million and over are up about 80% since last year.

The broker attributes the soaring luxury market to the end of a buying funk, when people were "thinking a bit more" about investing in waterfront properties due to storms. In fall 2024, Sarasota County had just caught its breath from Hurricane Helene when it was slammed by Hurricane Milton.

But apparently those hurricane hesitations have been swept away.

"Newer construction went through the storms unscathed," says Schemmel. "All of those people who were on the fence are like, 'If I want a house in Florida, why not buy now?'"

The typical multimillion-dollar estate buyer is already familiar with the area and upgrading, says the broker. Then there are the out-of-towners—mostly from the New York-New Jersey-CT tristate area—who are discovering Sarasota for its comparable affordability.

"We have a lot more waterfront options, and our price point is in a different zone from the East Coast and Naples," he says. "The value is here."

Not every property seems like a value play—Schemmel currently represents a $38.45 million 11-bedroom, 17,200-square-foot estate on Longboat Key—but that price tag would double or triple in Naples or Palm Beach.

The threat of erosion, storms, and climate change isn't stopping the wealthy from plunking down millions on properties mere feet from the water.

"Any time I'd think there would be an exodus from those barrier islands," he says. "I'd be proven wrong."

Kiri Blakeley writes about trending news at Realtor.com. She has also worked at Forbes Magazine, Forbes.com, CafeMom, and DailyMail.com, covering everything from billionaires to celebrities to crime. Her work can be found in news outlets worldwide, including Yahoo, SF Gate, New York Post, Seattle-Post Intelligencer, Marie Claire, She Knows, Huffington Post, and New York Magazine. She has an M.A. in journalism from Columbia University. In her spare time, she writes psychological thrillers under a pen name. She lives in Brooklyn, and her cat foster Instagram account has over 4 million views.

Artists' Modernist Houses

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A beautifully illustrated architecture book showcasing some of the most stunning Modernist houses which are or have been lived in by artists.

A beautifully illustrated architecture book showcasing some of the most stunning Modernist houses which are or have been lived in by artists.

This book is an enticing celebration of the most dynamic and unique architectural visions of artists who have designed their own houses, collaborated with or commissioned architects, or who have made major interventions into the existing architectural spaces where they live. Covering the mid to late 20th century, architectural journalist Harriet Thorpe catalogues 50 of the most extraordinary examples as well as the stories behind the artists who lived in them, demonstrating how visual art can inform and transform built living space.

Imaging Heaven

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Imaging Heaven is a visual narrative of frescoed church ceilings, photographed across Italy and Catholic Europe. Moving from the Proto-Renaissance to the Baroque, the book follows painters as they confront a fundamental problem: how to make the unseen realm of heaven perceptible amid profound shifts in cosmology, theology, and science.

Imaging Heaven is a visual narrative of frescoed church ceilings, photographed across Italy and Catholic Europe. Moving from the Proto-Renaissance to the Baroque, the book follows painters as they confront a fundamental problem: how to make the unseen realm of heaven perceptible amid profound shifts in cosmology, theology, and science.

Guided by threads of light, gesture, and illusion, the photographs reveal how painters moved from shadowless gold grounds rooted in Byzantine techniques to radiant, sophisticated illumination; turned hieratic icons into anatomically precise, emotionally charged bodies; and dissolved architectural boundaries with a photographic study of trompe-l’oeil, quadratura, and di sotto in sù effects that open ceilings onto boundless celestial space. Works by artists such as Correggio, Pozzo, and Tiepolo mark key moments in this ascent to Baroque theatricality.

Guided by threads of light, gesture, and illusion, the photographs reveal how painters moved from shadowless gold grounds rooted in Byzantine techniques to radiant, sophisticated illumination; turned hieratic icons into anatomically precise, emotionally charged bodies; and dissolved architectural boundaries with a photographic study of trompe-l’oeil, quadratura, and di sotto in sù effects that open ceilings onto boundless celestial space. Works by artists such as Correggio, Pozzo, and Tiepolo mark key moments in this ascent to Baroque theatricality.

Imaging Heaven situates these painted heavens within wider intellectual shifts—from Aristotelian and Ptolemaic cosmology to Copernican and Galilean discoveries, and from Reformation turmoil to Counter-Reformation efforts to move and persuade believers. In this expanded universe, ceiling frescoes relocate the hierarchical cosmos onto church vaults, transforming churches into sacred theaters where radiant light, illusionistic depth, and emotionally expressive figures create immersive and engaging encounters with the divine, turning an upward glance into an experience of transcendence.

Every Picture Tells a Story

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Hand drawing is arguably architecture's oldest "technology Even as we plunge headlong into the age of artificial intelligence, drawing remains a timeless, intuitive, and powerful medium for exploring. explaining, and understanding both built and imagined environments. A timeless tool for design thinking, hand drawing resonates on a deeply human level. This richly illustrated monograph features over 250 works from the 40-year career of architect Wesley L. Page, FAIA, ASAI, one of the nation's top traditional media architectural illustrators. Rather than a "how-to" book of drawing tricks and techniques, it is a visual journal organized around a purpose-based approach to drawing architecture and how understanding the overlap of drawing, design, and storytelling can profoundly impact the power and effectiveness of architectural Illustration. The drawings collectively represent a strategic approach to both conceiving of and executing drawings-not just drawing for illustration but drawing to achieve a more intentionally targeted outcome driven by story, purpose, and craft. Every Picture Tells a Story is a virtual encyclopedia that presents compelling, beautiful evidence of the lasting relevance, appeal, and storytelling capacity of hand drawing in the digital age.

Ronald Araujo agrees with Liverpool’s position on his £47m buy option

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Liverpool would prefer to wait until the end of the season before deciding whether or not to activate Ronald Araujo’s £47m buy option.

That’s the claim coming out of SPORT, with the defender himself also understood to prefer delaying a decision until the summer.

According to the outlet in question, the Reds are thought to be “very pleased” with the Uruguayan international. However, the club is very aware of the fact that it will take a somewhat significant figure to bring the centre-back to Anfield on a permanent basis.

But barring a sudden, unexpected collapse of truly cosmic proportions, it’s difficult to see how Liverpool could manage to justify not activating the buy option come the summer.

Even if the club’s long-term plan is to use a less conservative option at right-back, the fact of the matter is they’re going to be quite short on centre-back options come the end of the season.

Club skipper Virgil van Dijk (contracted until 2027) may yet sign an extension, but we can’t see Joe Gomez staying put beyond the summer window. Either way, and even assuming that Giovanni Leoni returns from his ACL injury in excellent shape and has a significant second half of the season at L4, the Reds will need to bolster the department to the tune of potentially two bodies.

Activating Araujo’s buy option, then, feels like a necessity rather than a luxury, especially given the experience that could walk out of the door at the end of the campaign.

SPORT go on to claim that Araujo “is aware that Barcelona is keeping a close eye on him”, which doesn’t really communicate a great deal as far as intention is concerned.

Prior reporting has indicated that Barca boss Hansi Flick will hold some sway in the player’s next move this summer, but ultimately, it should be emphasised that Liverpool Football Club handed the footballer a new lease of life. Not Barcelona. And we’d like to think that’ll count for something in the long run.

Strive (ASST) Adds 29 Bitcoin for $2.2 Million as Weekly Buys Slow After Crossing 43,000

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strive bought another 29 bitcoin last week for about $2.2 million at an average of $75,721 per coin between Oct. 31 and Nov. 6, a Monday 8-K filing shows, lifting total holdings to 43,153 BTC from 43,124 as of Oct. 30 and capping a month that had already pushed the Dallas treasury past 40,000 coins.

Chairman and CEO Matthew Cole posted the same figures on X: “Strive acquired 29 $BTC for $2.2M at an average cost of $75,721 per bitcoin, bringing total holdings to ₿43,153.” Strive’s corporate account republished the note minutes later. The average price is inclusive of fees and expenses, the filing says.

The buy is a sharp downshift from the pace Strive set through October. The prior week alone, from Oct. 24 through Oct. 30, Strive added 124 bitcoin at about $80,526 a coin. Between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30 the company acquired 15,124 bitcoin in total, and Friday’s third-quarter results put year-to-date bitcoin yield — Strive’s measure of the percentage change in bitcoin per share — at 63.2% through Sept. 30, with an 18.5% print for the third quarter alone.

Holdings stood at 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 before the October sprint. That climb followed multi-thousand-coin buys earlier this fall, including a 6,182-coin purchase funded with preferred proceeds, and kept Strive among the largest public corporate stacks after Strategy.

Cash and cash equivalents fell to $487.6 million as of Nov. 6 from $490.4 million a week earlier, a $2.8 million drop that roughly covers the roughly $2.2 million bitcoin spend plus fees. Strive did not break out a separate funding line for this week’s coins in the 8-K.

The preferred stack kept moving. Shares of Strive’s 15% Series A perpetual preferred (SATS) rose by 33,141 to 7,036,559, a tally that includes shares sold through 4:00 p.m. Eastern that settle the next business day. SATA preferred stock was unchanged at 14,030,176. Effective common shares outstanding held flat at 127,331,109 after a Class A increase of 15,382 was offset by a matching Class B decline.

Strive still holds 505,000 shares of Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred, marked at a fair value of $50.4 million as of Nov. 6, up slightly from $50.1 million a week earlier. The company closed a registered offering of 7 million SATS shares on Oct. 20 for about $609 million in net proceeds and later lined up a $1 billion SATS at-the-market program, tools that have funded the larger buys that built the stack and that still leave room to keep adding if the ATM finds buyers.

At bitcoin near $74,650, the 43,153-coin treasury is worth roughly $3.22 billion on paper. That marks the stack about 1.4% under this week’s $75,721 average purchase price, though Strive has not disclosed a companywide cost basis in Monday’s filing the way Strategy does each week.

The Strive update lands on the same Monday that Strategy disclosed its own weekly purchase. Strategy added 1,036 bitcoin for $81.9 million at an average of $79,012, lifting its holdings to 850,364 BTC bought for $64.16 billion at an average cost of $75,456. The two filings keep the familiar pairing in view: Strategy still dwarfs every other public corporate holder, while Strive has used preferred issuance to climb the treasury rankings with a faster bitcoin-per-share growth claim.

ASST closed Friday at $21.45, up from Thursday’s $20.59 close and still well below its Oct. 21 local high near $30.75. The 52-week range runs from $7.02 to $34.40. At the time of writing, bitcoin is near $74,650, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows. The test for Strive from here is whether another week of single-digit coin adds is a pause after October’s sprint or the new run rate once the preferred ATM is the main fuel.

Your Brand Is Your Most Powerful Differentiator

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Being an independent agency isn’t the advantage it used to be. There are simply too many choices.

And we can’t differentiate on capabilities anymore either. Most of us offer some combination of strategy, creative, media, production and now AI. And if we don’t have it all (and we’re smart), we have an explanation for how we can scale up when needed.

After more than four decades of leading agencies, I have some advice for my fellow founders and agency leaders: Don’t forget to invest in your own brand.

I know it sounds obvious. But I’ve lived through what happens when you let your own brand rest on its laurels because you’re busy building everyone else’s. The result is you end up having to work a lot harder to show people what you’re capable of, because they’ve already formed a perception.

So let’s invest in our brands so they can work for us. That starts by owning your story.

Ours began in 2004, when I led a management buyout to save our agency from disappearing. Our original 40 employees, who we still call the ‘Brave 40,’ believed in what we were building and decided to stay, and they even pooled their own money to buy the sign that would hang above our door.

We were a little agency from Rochester, New York, determined to make the industry see that great ideas can come from anywhere. We were scrappy and smart, and a little rascally. The kind of agency you never saw coming.

That spirit is still in us, but we’ve grown up a lot over the last 22 years, too. In 2012, we sold to Project Worldwide to gain the ability to scale, because their Detroit roots matched our grit, and because their independent and employee-owned model allowed us to keep our soul. As we grew, we raised the bar across our business, from the talent we attracted, to our work, and the brands we worked with.

Along the way, our brand stopped keeping up with who we’d become, and it also lost the soul that gave us our start. We needed to reflect the agency we’d become while protecting the equity and reputation we’d built.

Now, here’s something that might surprise you: I didn’t lead our rebrand.

I’ve always believed in leading from a place of abundance, and making room for more people and ideas. That means trusting the people around you to see things you might not.

I let go, and the rest of the leadership team led the rebrand with the freedom to challenge everything, including the name on the door.

Keeping my name wasn’t part of the brief. In fact, my only request was that they not call us P+N.

The team brought in outside consultants to challenge their thinking and tested the new positioning with search consultants to understand what resonated and what needed work.

One piece of feedback came through over and over: Whatever you’re saying, go further. Don’t be afraid to be provocative.

It was a good reminder that in an industry where we’re all selling creativity in one way or another, how we tell our own story is our lifeline. We can’t afford to sound like everyone else.

So while many agencies are moving away from founder names in favor of names that describe what they deliver, we went the other direction.

The team chose NAPIER because it represented a mindset and a spirit that had been with us since 2004. That founder who risked everything because she believed in her team, and the people who stuck by her to bring it to life. We’ve always had to outthink, outwork and outshine agencies many times our size. And now we’re owning our roots in a new way, with a simpler name that carries more power.

We also got much clearer about who we’re for. We’re not going after every challenger brand, we’re focused on “sleeping giants”: brands that have strong equity or scale, but aren’t realizing their full potential. These are brands who need to break free from familiar patterns and move differently. It’s what we’ve always done best, but now we have a sharper and more interesting way to articulate it.

On October 22, after 22 years as Partners + Napier, we officially unveiled our new name, visual identity and positioning. It’s been surreal to watch it all come to life, and it’s also proved what I’ve long believed about leading with abundance.

You have to know what makes you special, but you also have to give others room to see possibilities you might not. That’s how you build something that can keep growing beyond you and actually become a legacy.

We are in the business of building brands. We challenge clients to have conviction, to evolve, to get clearer about who they are and to make choices that will set them apart.

So to my fellow agency leaders: Don’t wait for a major business disruption to take a fresh look at your brand. And don’t assume that because you know what makes your agency special, everyone else does too.

Give your own brand the attention you’d give your most important client.

And give the people you’ve brought into your business the room to make it even better.

Honored by Ad Age as one of the 100 Most Influential Women in Advertising, Sharon Napier has earned her reputation as one of the most trusted voices in the creative industry. She serves as Founder of NAPIER, an integrated creative agency, twice ranked among the Top 20 Most Effective Agencies in North America by Effie Worldwide. In addition to her role at NAPIER, Sharon also serves as Senior Advisor, Global Strategic Alliances at Project Worldwide -- an independent and employee-owned holding company for ambitious agencies. Sharon is a champion of independent agencies, women in leadership, and the future of talent – sharing her perspectives on global stages including Advertising Week, the Cannes Lions Festival of Creativity, the Ad Age Small Agency Conference, the ANA Financial Management Conference, and many others. She has contributed insights to publications and podcasts including The New York Times, The Wall Street Journal, Fast Company, Ad Age, Adweek, Campaign, MediaPost, OWN IT, Great Minds, and more. In recognition of her contributions, Sharon was recently inducted into the Rochester Business Hall of Fame and has received numerous accolades including the ATHENA Award, the Adweek Female Trailblazer Award, and Working Mother of the Year from She Runs It. Sharon serves as Interim Board Chair of the 4A’s, sits on the Board of Trustees at Rochester Institute of Technology, and is a member of the Board of Directors at the Memorial Art Gallery.

The Design Village: Educating the Next Generation of Designers for Social Transformation

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

What if a design school were designed the way we hope designers will one day design the world: starting from people, place, planet and purpose rather than from convention?In 2011, a small group of practiconers and academics set out to answer that question on the outskirts of Delhi. The result was The Design Village (TDV), an independent design school in Noida, India. It was built from first principles as a response to the gaps in design education and it now has a European campus in Lombardy, Italy. This book tells the story of that experiment. It is part manifesto, part memoir, and part working guide to design education of the 21st Century.

What if a design school were designed the way we hope designers will one day design the world: starting from people, place, planet and purpose rather than from convention?In 2011, a small group of practiconers and academics set out to answer that question on the outskirts of Delhi. The result was The Design Village (TDV), an independent design school in Noida, India. It was built from first principles as a response to the gaps in design education and it now has a European campus in Lombardy, Italy. This book tells the story of that experiment. It is part manifesto, part memoir, and part working guide to design education of the 21st Century.

Co-authors Mridu Sahai Patnaik, Paul Hekkert - co-founders of TDV, trace how a school can be organised around three convictions. It can be impact-first, measuring success by what students do for society. It can be pedagogy-centric, treating how we learn as a design problem in its own right. And it can be unreasonably reciprocal, building a learning community where students, faculty, industry and the neighbourhood all give to and learn from each other.Promoting a non silo approach, the book follows real studios, projects and villaging (character building). It looks honestly at the tension between idealism and running a school, and at what it takes to keep an educational vision intact as it grows across continents.With a foreword by design theorist Don Norman, the book is meant for educators, architects, designers, students and anyone who believes that the way we train designers shapes the cities, objects and systems we will all live with. It is an invitation to see design education not as preparation for a profession but as a practice of social transformation.

The ghosts of Charles Dickens’ A Christmas Carol—Past, Present, and Future— are well known. But this holiday season, Xfinity is introducing a fourth: The Ghost of Christmas Very-Recent-Past.

And this ghost has an unusually specific power: Showing those distracted by their devices what they just missed in real life.

In a campaign that launches today (Nov. 9), the Ghost of Christmas Very-Recent-Past is played by Academy Award-nominated actor John C. Reilly. The spot, directed by Jim Jenkins and created in partnership with Goodby Silverstein & Partners, features several vignettes of holiday gatherings where someone has missed a special memory.

In the first, two young people kiss under a mistletoe while the crowd “aws.”

Coinbase Wins Full Federal Trust Charter From OCC

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Coinbase has won a full national trust bank charter from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, upgrading the conditional approval it received in April and putting its institutional custody business under direct federal supervision, the exchange said Monday.

The OCC approved the application for Coinbase National Trust Company last Friday, the company said in a blog post. OCC licensing records, as reported, show the chartering process was completed Nov. 6.

Coinbase applied in October 2025 and received preliminary approval on April 2. In the intervening months it had to finish its organizational and operational preparations before the OCC would let the trust bank open.

“This charter allows us to bring clear and consistent federal uniformity to our industry-leading custody capabilities,” Coinbase wrote, adding that it strengthens the regulatory framework under which it serves customers. The company said it has served as a custodian for more than 14 years.

A national trust bank can hold assets for clients as a fiduciary, and Coinbase’s focus is custody for institutional customers. It is not a commercial bank. It cannot take ordinary retail deposits or make loans, and the charter does not authorize it to issue stablecoins.

Staking, prime trading and financing will keep running through Coinbase affiliates, not the trust itself. Under the OCC’s April decision, the trust bank acts as an intermediary for those services and does not perform them directly.

The decision also sets conditions. The trust bank must hold at least $60 million in Tier 1 capital and enough liquid assets to cover 180 days of operating expenses during its first three years.

The license does not automatically connect Coinbase to Federal Reserve payment systems either. That requires a separate application under the Fed’s account access guidelines.

Coinbase already runs custody through Coinbase Custody Trust Company, a limited-purpose trust regulated by the New York State Department of Financial Services. The company plans to move that business to the national entity over three years.

Coinbase is the latest crypto firm to complete a federal trust charter. Ripple received final approval on Oct. 26, and Circle won final OCC approval in July, a decision that put USDC reserves on a path toward federal supervision.

The OCC has been courting the sector. In August, Comptroller Jonathan V. Gould said “America and the OCC are once again open for business,” as the regulator touted its charter push. Ripple, Circle, Crypto.com and Paxos had all received conditional approval by then. Block applied for its own banking charter in September.

The approval did not come without resistance. The Independent Community Bankers of America urged the OCC in December to reject Coinbase’s application, arguing the exchange has “demonstrably flawed risk and control functions.” The American Bankers Association asked the regulator in February to slow its review of crypto charter requests.

Underneath those objections is a turf war over stablecoins. Coinbase wants to pay customers rewards for holding the tokens, and banks say that could erode their deposit base.

Coinbase (COIN) did not disclose any financial impact from the charter in its announcement, which framed the approval as a regulatory milestone.

The timing is notable. The approval arrives days after Democrats won both chambers of Congress, and a charter issued by an executive-branch regulator sits outside any legislative fight, at least for now. Vice Chairman Ryan VanGrack said the company has spent years “asking for clear rules and rigorous oversight.” The company also said the approval is “another major step” in integrating digital assets into the American financial system.

The next test is whether Coinbase moves its existing custody clients over on schedule, and whether the community bankers who opposed the charter take their objections to court or to Congress.

Man Reportedly Stabbed in Neck During Halloween Party at Jamie Foxx's Home

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A Halloween party at Jamie Foxx’s California home reportedly turned violent after a man was stabbed in the neck.

A partygoer who attended Foxx and daughter Anelise Foxx’s annual Skvlk Fest event at the star’s Thousand Oaks mansion on Saturday, November 7, was treated for a superficial neck wound, TMZ reported on Sunday, November 8, citing law enforcement sources.

A male suspect was reportedly arrested for assault with a deadly weapon, per the outlet.

Us has reached out to the Ventura County Sheriff’s Office for comment.

Neither Foxx, 58, nor his daughter has publicly commented on the alleged incident.

On Sunday, the Oscar winner shared several clips from the festival via Instagram. “@skvlkfest @anelisefoxx was 🔥🔥🔥. @djsophia_ you killed it once again!!” he captioned one post.

Foxx shares daughter Anelise, 18, with ex Kristin Grannis. He also shares eldest daughter Corinne Foxx, 32, with ex Connie Kline.

News broke earlier this month that the actor had welcomed his third child with girlfriend Alyce Huckstepp.

Foxx first opened up about becoming a dad again back in August.

“Being my age, I was a little on the fence,” he told E! News, adding that he changed his perspective after having a conversation with Al Pacino, who welcomed son Roman, 3, in 2023 at age 83.

Foxx said, “But then I ran into Al Pacino a few months ago. ‘I said, ‘Al, you gonna be back in the drop-off line?’ I said, ‘Aw man, shoot, I’m good.’”

The Back in Action star recently praised Corinne for helping him recover after he suffered a stroke in 2023. A year later, he said the stroke was caused by a brain bleed.

“When you look at The Godfather and Michael Corleone is out with his girl on Christmas, he picks up the newspaper and sees that his father has been shot, and he says, ‘It doesn’t say if he’s alive,’ and he’s not really telling his girl what he’s reading,” Foxx said of Corinne during an August appearance on the “Baby, It’s Keke Palmer” podcast. “Michael Corleone left and he went to that hospital, and he took over. He told one of the guys, ‘There were people coming here to do danger to my father. I need you to help me.’ That’s who she was.”

“When I talked to the doctor, I said, ‘Hey, listen. I hear everybody’s stories. I want to know from you what it was,’” he recalled. “He said, ‘Hey man, without her, you wouldn’t be here.’ So I said, ‘Hey, that’s Michael Corleone forever.’”

Foxx added, “The doctor was like, ‘No one handles it like this … because you were on the slab, TMZ, all these different [people were asking for details], and she handled it.’”

Another week, another batch of books for your TBR pile. Happy reading, folks.

Brendan Flaherty, Middleman (Atlantic Crime)

“A delightfully strange and satisfying story of a man whose life has fallen to pieces and the mysteries that lie at the heart of this devastation. Dwelling on the edge of madcap insanity, Middleman never loses its narrative steam.” –Booklist

“For lovers of cats, podcasts, and the insides of other people’s homes, The Terrarium is a relatable thriller about the gig economy gone wrong. I was so excited to see what (or who) was kept inside those glass cages that I finished it in a single day. Twisty, fun, and a little scary.” –Tasha Coryel

“Wrobel (The Hitchcock Hotel) brings her literary A-game to this double-layered puzzler that deftly mines the craft of fiction writing and the business of publishing for spectacularly entertaining results.” –Library Journal

Even if they guess the who, the hows and whys keep The Cold Read boiling all the way to the end. An absorbing and enjoyable ride.” –-Booklist,

J.A. Jance, The Taken Ones (William Morrow)

“Nearly four decades into her career, Jance is still finding new ways to thrill her readers.” –Publishers Weekly

Caitlin Starling, Milkteeth (St Martin’s)

“Starling’s take on the toll of motherhood, and breastfeeding specifically, on the body and psyche is delightfully ruthless and unsparing. Fans will not be disappointed.” –Publishers Weekly

51 Years Later, Jaws' Most Famous Line Is Still The Greatest Quote In Movie History

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

How many movies can you name that inspire such a massive, visceral reaction with such a minimalist approach? "Jaws" stands on an island unto itself in this regard, practically since the moment the blockbuster-defining hit first made landfall in 1975. The one-word title alone is a stroke of genius, standing in stark contrast to clunky word salads that studios tend to go with these days (usually followed by an obnoxious subtitle of some kind, naturally). John Williams' iconic score and that famous two-note theme quite literally speak for themselves, evoking a sense of dread and menace that would've been lost amid a more overbearing arrangement.

But most of all, despite including a barnburner monologue or two, let's just say there's a reason why one of the simplest and most straightforward lines of all has gone on to become arguably the most famous quote in cinema history. Of course, we're talking about actor Roy Scheider's pitch-perfect portrayal of police chief Martin Brody's moment of realization out at sea: "You're gonna need a bigger boat." Celebrated and parodied and recreated countless times since in pop culture, this one piece of dialogue towers above all the rest.

It's fun to imagine those in the theater on opening day actually feeling their moviegoing reality shift around them during this scene, just as the action in "Jaws" itself shifted into another gear entirely. The truth is, however, this was one of those singular moments in cinema that needed time and perspective to fully appreciate. It's not hyperbolic in the least to state that everything changed after this, in ways that are still reverberating today. Over 50 years later, it's as good a time as any to look back and appreciate the greatest quote of them all.

"You're gonna need a bigger boat." — Martin Brody

Have six total words ever encapsulated so many primal emotions? We're not exactly reinventing the wheel to propose that "Jaws" taps into an instinctive fear of the unknown and the horrors of nature, lurking just outside our comfortable existence. Despite how high we rise and how far our greedy ambitions take us, we're still fragile beings at the mercy of forces beyond our control — whether it's hapless swimmers trying to enjoy summer vacation, or, say, a certain mayor of Amity Island concerned solely about tourism profits over public safety. And when a killer apex predator is on the prowl, well, not even a shark hunter's boat is enough to keep us safe in unfamiliar waters.

That's the psychoanalyzing version of events, at least. While sitting down to experience "Jaws" for the umpteenth re-watch (or even for the very first time), what sticks out the most in this classic scene is just how terrified everyone is once they finally catch a glimpse of their quarry. Even Robert Shaw's cold-blooded Quint, a man who's seen and survived the worst, is visibly taken aback by the sight of a gigantic dorsal fin breaching the surface. In the moment, this isn't an existential concern about humanity's place in the food chain. This is pure survival, plain and simple.

Amusingly enough, there's another alternate explanation behind this legendary line. In a now-famous production story, it's been revealed that this was actually an improvised insult aimed squarely at penny-pinching producers. As co-writer Carl Gottlieb explained, "['You're gonna need a bigger boat'] became a catchphrase for anytime anything went wrong." With that added layer of irony, it's no wonder this quote took on a life of its own.

The Queens Public Library system is being held for ransom

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In a bizarre turn of events, on Wednesday November 4th, New York City’s Queens Public Library (QPL)  system (which operates 66 branches), experienced an attack on its online networks by a hacker. The entire system is offline, and is being held for ransom.

According to the QPL, all branches remain open, but online networks are down, resulting in disruptions to the library website, on-site computers, check-out and return machines, wi-fi, the library’s mobile app, and all digital media services.

It has not been disclosed what or how much the hackers are asking in return for releasing the system. It is also unknown who is behind the ransomware attack.

QPL CEO Dennis Walcott told Gothamist, “Unfortunately, it’s part of the world that we live in right now. So the Queens Public Library is a victim of the type of world that we’re living in, where people are launching cyber attacks against various types of entities, large corporations, and now Queens Public Library.”

His remark highlights the absolute strangeness of the events… the QPL is hardly a “large corporation.” In fact, it’s a very small nonprofit. It’s not even New York’s largest library system. The NYPL, which services Manhattan, the Bronx, and Staten Island, operates 92 locations. (For those wondering, the Brooklyn Public Library system operates 62 branches.)

But also… it’s a library system! It’s whole business is to provide widespread access to books, additional literary and media resources, internet access, printing services, education opportunities, and community and children’s programming to people. FOR FREE. What the hell type of jerk goes after a library system, especially a library system in one of the most ethnically and linguistically diverse areas in the world? (As a Queens native and huge QPL fan, but also just a person who thinks books are good, I’m furious.)

Walcott also noted that it is currently unknown if user information has been compromised.

However, QPL users can still check books in and out manually (it is a library, after all). So, analog tech has saved the day.

Stay tuned for more updates, and long live libraries!

She has a PhD from the departments of English/comparative literature and theatre at Columbia University, where she was the Marion E. Ponsford fellow. Art, syrup, a legendary stone: Maxie Dara’s 3 favorite heists of the last century

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Crime is a subject often synonymous with tragedy, with strife, with human suffering. But sometimes it’s also a little goofy.

I spent my first two books, A Grim Reaper’s Guide to Catching a Killer and A Grim Reaper’s Guide to Cheating Death, focused on the murder of it all. My search history probably has me on at least one watch list and I can give you a handful of creative ways to kill somebody with a glitter pen if you ask nicely.

But for my third dive back into my world of death with A Grim Reaper’s Guide to Living Twice, I decided to play against type and go for a silly little heist instead. And it turns out I’m not alone. It fact, it turns out recent history is full of silly little heists that take the concept of a crime and turn it into a certified shenanigan. From student hijinks to condiment capers, here are my top three favorite heists of the last century.

Come with me to my home nation of Canada, to my birth province of Quebec, where poutine is plentiful and everybody’s at least a little disappointed in your lack of French fluency. It also happens to be a hub of maple syrup production, though that feels like something of an understatement. According to The Canadian Encyclopedia, “In 2020, Quebec contributed 96.4 per cent of Canadian maple product exports.”

With all that sweet tree nectar flowing, it may come as no surprise to you (though it genuinely did to me) to learn that the province keeps a syrup stockpile in the form of the Strategic Maple Syrup Reserve. Think of it as a sort of Fort Knox, but instead of gold it’s filled with pancake toppings, which is way more appealing in my opinion. And I’m not the only one who thinks so.

Between 2011 and 2012, a group of sticky-fingered (sorry) Reserve employees decided to help themselves to the stock in order to sell it to other provinces and states. This slow-motion heist involved months of siphoning maple syrup out of heavy barrels. The thieves would then either replace their loot with water or leave the barrels empty. Unfortunately for the robbers, there’s a bit of a difference between an empty barrel and one filled with syrup—a six-hundred-pound difference, in fact.

When an inspector for the organization in charge of the Reserve tried to climb up a stack of these supposedly heavy barrels only for one to nearly topple under his weight, he became just a smidge suspicious. Sure enough, a subsequent investigation found that around three thousand tonnes of maple syrup had been stolen, its value a staggering $18 million.

The investigation also uncovered a crime ring comprised of syrup industry insiders, from truck drivers to sugar shack workers, who had rented a separate space in the same warehouse as the Reserve so they could perpetrate their crimes unnoticed. Though my favourite fact about this entire case is that a maple syrup black market exists, and the ringleader of the operation, Richard Vallières, had already been a part of it long before this heist. Some criminal underworlds just sound more delicious than others, you know?

Kids these days, with their music and their haircuts and their schemes to steal back ancient symbols of Scottish heritage.

It’s Christmas in 1950 and four Glasgow University students have decided to crank their youthful rebellion up a notch. While other twenty-somethings are sleeping off hangovers or opening presents with their families, Ian Hamilton, Gavin Vernon, Kay Matheson and Alan Stuart are plotting to retrieve the Stone of Scone.

Also known as the Stone of Destiny, the roughly three-hundred-and-thirty-six-pound block of red sandstone had been an important part of Scottish coronations for hundred of years before King Edward I stole it for England during the 13th century. During its long history the stone was imbued with rich legends, connecting its heritage all the way back to the first king of Scotland and making it an important national symbol. South of the border it continued to be used in coronations, this time siting beneath each English monarch as they were crowned, but its importance to Scotland remained.

Centuries after its initial theft by King Edward I, the four plucky students decided to reclaim the stone in an act of national pride. The group drove south and waited until midnight to steal the Stone of Scone from where it now lay in Westminster Abbey.

With Kay on guard duty, the three men tried to hoist the stone to freedom, but it broke in two during their attempt. Ian took the smaller piece while Gavin and Alan took the larger and fled in a separate car. Back with Kay, Ian soon found himself in the crosshairs of a passing officer, but in an Ocean’s Eleven-like twist, the students disguised their heist as a youthful makeout session and managed to get away.

The story only gets more wild from there, with the stone halves finding themselves hidden under floorboards and buried under a campsite before they were eventually reunited. And while it did end up returning to England after the students were caught, the Stone of Scone was given back to Scotland in 1996, and none of the young thieves faced legal consequences for their actions.

The Louvre is no stranger to an entertaining heist. In 1911, a man called Vincenzo Peruggia dressed as a museum worker and snatched the Mona Lisa in broad daylight. Unfortunately for the Louvre but very fortunately for schadenfreude fans everywhere, the museum art minister was on holiday at the time and before he left he had apparently insisted, “don’t call me unless the Louvre burns down or the Joconde [French for Mona Lisa] is stolen.”

Fast forward just shy of a hundred years and this same level of absolute tomfoolery was on display again. In 2025, two men dressed as construction workers, hoisted themselves up to a museum balcony using a furniture lift and stole €88 million worth of French Crown Jewels in under eight minutes. Though the culprits were eventually caught, the heist will live on in infamy for both its devious efficiency and the frankly top tier memes it inspired.

Maxie Dara is a writer and actor from Ontario, Canada. She has been a freelance journalist focusing on the local arts and culture scene for more than five years, with bylines in publications such as Hamilton Magazine and Beyond James, among others. She is also a two-time award-winning playwright, taking home the Best of Fringe award at the 2017 Hamilton Fringe Festival for the musical comedy This Is Not a Musical: The Musical! and the 2020 Torpedo Prize for her play Alone Together, a pandemic drama. Maxie knew she wanted to be a writer at the age of seven, when she first fell in love with the written word. She also wanted to be a mermaid but has mostly focused on the writing side of things.
